These are the complete versions (~2,200 words each), so you can see every move. In the exam (~55 min, ~1,500 words), keep every Application paragraph and shorten the Law sections: one line of facts per case, and in a public-authority answer only the cases you then compare.

A. 2026 Semester 1, Q1: Oliver v Skyline Events (25 marks), the most recent paper

Question text (click)

Oliver attends a large outdoor music festival organised by Skyline Events (Skyline). The event includes a main stage, several temporary stages and multiple food vendors. Skyline engages SoundCo to install and manage the sound system for the festival.

At around 2pm on the first day of the festival, several attendees notice that a section of temporary flooring near the main stage has become unstable, causing it to shift when walked on. Two attendees report the issue to Skyline staff. In response, a Skyline staff member inspects the area and posts a warning on the festival's official Facebook page advising patrons to take care near the main stage due to uneven flooring. No physical warning signs are placed at the site and no repairs are carried out at the time due to a shortage of staff.

Later that evening, Oliver walks across the uneven section of flooring after consuming several alcoholic drinks. As he approaches the area, he is looking towards the stage and not paying attention to the ground in front of him. As he steps onto the uneven section, the surface shifts beneath him, causing him to lose his balance and he begins to fall. At that moment, a technical malfunction with the equipment operated by SoundCo causes a loud and unexpected burst of noise from one of the speakers. Startled by the noise and already unbalanced, Oliver reacts quickly to try and steady himself, but twists his body awkwardly and falls heavily.

Oliver suffers a serious back injury requiring extended hospitalisation. Medical evidence later suggests that Oliver's injuries were caused by a combination of the unstable flooring and his sudden reaction to the noise. During his hospitalisation, he is prescribed strong pain medication for a prolonged period. Although taken in accordance with medical advice, Oliver develops an addiction to the medication and requires a rehabilitation program to treat his addiction. The rehabilitation program costs $5,000. Oliver also incurs significant medical expenses ($36,000) and a loss of six months' income due to his inability to work. His average annual salary is $95,000.

Oliver comes to you seeking advice against Skyline. He wishes to make a claim for damages under Tort Law. Advise Oliver. Please use ILAC to answer this question.

Answer plan (write this in planning time)
  1. Duty: occupier/organiser → patron (Zaluzna; Donoghue neighbour). Not a public authority. Short.
  2. Breach: s 9(1)(a) known at 2pm (2 reports + inspection); (b) shifting floor + crowds; (c) signs, cordon, repair. s 9(2): probability high; serious; burden low (staff shortage no excuse); social utility ≠ Watt. Facebook-only warning inadequate. Distinguish Bolton.
  3. Causation: (a) back injury + $36k medical + $47.5k income: but-for + multiple causes (SoundCo noise) → material contribution (Chappel, Cook); not a Yates-style break; scope OK (Overseas Tankships). (b) $5k rehab: distinguish Yates and Schneider (prescribed).
  4. Defences: contributory negligence: intoxication (Manley) + not looking (Ingram); ~25%. VAR fails (no knowledge; Facebook; not obvious).
  5. Remedies: $36,000 + $5,000 + $47,500 = $88,500 (+ pain and suffering, future loss); −25% → $66,375. Conclusion.

Whether Oliver can recover damages from Skyline Events ('Skyline') depends on whether he can establish each element of the tort of negligence (duty, breach and causation) and whether any defences reduce Skyline's liability.

Issue

Can Oliver bring an action against Skyline for the tort of negligence?

Law: Negligence generally

A tort is a civil wrong that allows an injured person to sue the wrongdoer for damages. A person commits the tort of negligence if they carelessly cause harm to another. To succeed, Oliver must establish on the balance of probabilities that: (1) Skyline owed him a duty of care; (2) Skyline breached that duty; and (3) the breach caused his loss, which falls within the scope of Skyline's liability. Defences and remedies must then be considered. The common law applies together with the Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld) ('CLA').

1. Duty of care

Law

In Donoghue v Stevenson [1932] AC 562, a manufacturer was held to owe a duty of care to the ultimate consumer of a ginger beer containing a decomposed snail. Lord Atkin's 'neighbour principle' requires reasonable care to avoid acts or omissions that one can reasonably foresee would be likely to injure one's 'neighbour', being persons so closely and directly affected that they ought reasonably to be in contemplation. Later cases recognised established categories of duty. Relevantly, an occupier of premises owes a duty of care to all persons entering the premises to ensure that the premises are safe: Australian Safeway Stores Pty Ltd v Zaluzna (1987).

Application

Skyline organised the festival and controlled the site, including the temporary flooring near the main stage. It was therefore the occupier. Oliver entered the site as a festival patron (it is assumed he held a valid ticket). The relationship falls within the established occupier–entrant category in Zaluzna. It was also reasonably foreseeable that carelessness about the safety of the flooring could injure a patron walking near the main stage, so Oliver was Skyline's 'neighbour'. Skyline is a private event organiser, not a public authority, so the hidden-risk analysis applicable to public authorities is not required.

Margin note: keep established-category duty SHORT. Feedback criticised students who wandered into novel-duty "salient features".

Conclusion

Skyline owed Oliver a duty of care.

2. Breach of duty

Law

A duty of care is not automatically breached. Under s 9(1) CLA, a person breaches their duty by failing to take precautions against a risk of harm if (a) the risk was foreseeable, being one they knew or ought reasonably to have known about; (b) the risk was not insignificant; and (c) in the circumstances a reasonable person in their position would have taken those precautions. Under s 9(2), the court considers (a) the probability of harm, (b) the likely seriousness of the harm, (c) the burden of taking precautions and (d) the social utility of the activity. In Paris v Stepney Borough Council (1951) an employer breached its duty by failing to give goggles to a worker blind in one eye, because harm to him would be so serious. In Watt v Hertfordshire County Council (1954) the social utility of rushing to save a life justified the risk of carrying unsecured equipment, so there was no breach.

In Bolton v Stone (1951), a cricket ball was hit out of the ground and struck a woman on the road. Because balls had very rarely left the ground over many years, the probability of harm was very low and the burden of eliminating the risk was high, so a reasonable person would not have taken further precautions and there was no breach.

Application

Foreseeability (s 9(1)(a)). Skyline actually knew of the risk. By about 2pm, two attendees had reported the unstable flooring and a Skyline staff member inspected it and acknowledged the danger by posting a warning about 'uneven flooring'. The risk was therefore foreseeable.

Not insignificant (s 9(1)(b)). The flooring shifted when walked on and was located near the main stage, the busiest part of a large festival, through which many patrons would pass. The risk was not insignificant.

Reasonable person (s 9(1)(c) and s 9(2)). A reasonable festival organiser would have cordoned off the area, placed physical warning signs and repaired or replaced the section. The s 9(2) factors support this:

  • Probability (a): The probability of harm was high. Large crowds would walk across the area for the rest of the day and into the evening, often in poor light, looking at the stage rather than the ground, and in many cases after drinking alcohol.
  • Seriousness (b): A fall on shifting flooring in a crowd can cause serious injuries such as back and head injuries, as in fact occurred. As in Paris v Stepney, the seriousness of the potential harm demanded more than minimal precautions.
  • Burden (c): The burden was low. Barrier tape, a physical sign or rerouting patrons would have been quick and inexpensive. Skyline may argue that a staff shortage made repairs impossible at the time. However, staffing levels were within Skyline's own control, and even if repairs had to wait, cordoning the area or placing a sign required minimal resources.
  • Social utility (d): A music festival has entertainment and commercial value, but unlike the emergency rescue in Watt v Hertfordshire, nothing urgent justified leaving a known hazard in place.

Skyline will argue that it took reasonable precautions by posting a warning on its official Facebook page. However, patrons already on site, watching performances in the evening, are unlikely to check the festival's Facebook page. The warning also did not identify the exact location and left the hazard in place, so it was not what a reasonable person would have done.

This case is distinguishable from Bolton v Stone. There, the harm was an extremely rare event that the defendant could not practically prevent. Here, the danger was specific, reported, inspected and likely to cause injury, and could have been avoided at little cost.

Margin note: the "Similar to / Unlike Bolton v Stone" sentence is the case analysis the feedback says HD answers do. Always compare the probability AND the burden.

Conclusion

Skyline breached its duty of care to Oliver under s 9 CLA.

3. Causation

Law

Under s 11(1) CLA, a breach causes harm if (a) the breach was a necessary condition of the harm (factual causation) and (b) it is appropriate for the scope of the defendant's liability to extend to the harm (scope of liability). Factual causation is usually tested with the 'but for' test. However, where harm has several causes, the court applies the 'material contribution' test: whether the defendant's conduct increased the risk of injury and that risk eventuated. The breach need not be the sole cause: Chappel v Hart (1998). In Cook v ACT Racing Club Inc [2001], an overheated sauna that dehydrated a jockey was held to have caused his heart attack even though dehydration was only one cause, because it significantly increased the risk of injury. An intervening act may break the chain of causation. In Yates v Jones [1990], a plaintiff injured in a car accident became addicted to heroin suggested by a friend while in hospital, and the addiction was held to have been caused by the friend, not the accident.

Under the scope of liability test, the type of harm must be a reasonably foreseeable consequence of the breach and not too remote: Overseas Tankships v Morts [1961]. In Schneider v Smith & Anor (2016), a plaintiff injured in a motorcycle accident deliberately turned to illegal drugs outside medical advice. This was held to be a voluntary, unreasonable act not forming part of prescribed treatment, so causation failed for the drug use. However, medical and psychological treatment costs were recoverable as the 'ordinary and natural consequences' of the accident.

Application

(a) Back injury, medical expenses ($36,000) and lost income.

Factual causation: But for Skyline's failure to repair, cordon off or signpost the flooring, Oliver would not have stepped onto the unstable section, lost his balance and begun to fall. However, the medical evidence shows that his injuries were caused by a combination of the unstable flooring and his sudden reaction to the noise from SoundCo's equipment. Because there are multiple causes, the material contribution test is more appropriate. Skyline's breach plainly increased the risk of injury, and that risk eventuated: Oliver was already unbalanced and falling because of the flooring when the noise occurred. Similar to Cook v ACT Racing Club, where the overheated sauna was only one cause of the heart attack, the flooring need not be the sole cause for Skyline to be liable.

Skyline may argue that SoundCo's malfunction was an intervening act that broke the chain of causation, as the friend's suggestion did in Yates v Jones. This argument is weak. Unlike Yates, where a third party's independent and later choice caused a separate harm (the addiction), here the noise occurred 'at that moment' while Oliver was already falling, and combined with the existing danger to produce a single injury. Further, a loud noise from speakers at a music festival is not unforeseeable, and SoundCo was engaged by Skyline itself. (Oliver may also have a separate claim against SoundCo for its malfunctioning equipment, but this advice is confined to Skyline.)

Scope of liability: A serious back injury is exactly the type of harm that is reasonably foreseeable from a patron falling on unstable flooring. Hospitalisation, medical expenses and loss of income while unable to work are the ordinary and natural consequences of that injury, as recognised in Schneider. Unlike the unforeseeable fire damage in Overseas Tankships, this harm is not remote.

(b) Addiction and rehabilitation program ($5,000).

Factual causation: But for the back injury, Oliver would not have been hospitalised or prescribed strong pain medication, and would not have become addicted. The breach is therefore an indirect but necessary condition of the rehabilitation cost.

Scope of liability: Skyline will rely on Yates v Jones and Schneider v Smith to argue the addiction is too remote. Both cases are distinguishable. In Yates, the addiction was to heroin suggested by a friend, which was an independent act of a third party. In Schneider, the plaintiff deliberately took illegal drugs without reporting his symptoms or following medical advice. By contrast, Oliver took medication that was prescribed to him in hospital and took it 'in accordance with medical advice'. There was no deliberate, voluntary or unreasonable act on his part to break the chain of causation. Consistent with Schneider, where treatment costs were recoverable as ordinary and natural consequences, dependence on prescribed pain relief after a serious injury and the cost of treating it are reasonably foreseeable consequences of Skyline's breach.

Margin note: applying BOTH tests separately to EACH head of damage under headings is what separated HD from Credit in the Tut Sub 2 feedback.

Conclusion

Skyline's breach caused Oliver's back injury, medical expenses, lost income and rehabilitation costs, all of which fall within the scope of its liability.

4. Defences

Law

If a plaintiff contributed in some way to their own injury, liability is apportioned between the parties and damages reduced (contributory negligence): Ingram v Britten [1994], where the plaintiff drove a tractor at speed but the defendant had failed to secure its metal frame; Manley v Alexander, where the plaintiff drank 12 stubbies and slept on the road before being negligently driven over. Voluntary assumption of risk is a complete defence but is very difficult to establish. The plaintiff must have had full knowledge and appreciation of the precise risk and freely accepted it: Agar v Hyde (2000). Under ss 13–15 CLA, a plaintiff is presumed aware of an obvious risk, and there is no duty to warn of one.

Application

Contributory negligence: Skyline will argue that Oliver contributed to his injury. He had consumed several alcoholic drinks and was looking towards the stage rather than at the ground in front of him. Similar to Manley v Alexander, where the plaintiff's intoxication contributed to his injury, Oliver's drinking likely affected his balance and awareness. Like the plaintiff in Ingram v Britten, he failed to take reasonable care for his own safety. On the other hand, the flooring shifted unexpectedly, and patrons at a music festival can be expected to look at the stage. Skyline's failure to address a known hazard therefore remains the primary cause. If Oliver had read the Facebook warning, his responsibility would be greater, but the facts do not say this and it is assumed he had not. On balance, a reduction of around 25% is likely.

Voluntary assumption of risk: This defence is unlikely to succeed. Unlike the rugby players in Agar v Hyde, who knew the inherent risks of their sport, there is no evidence Oliver knew the flooring was unstable. The only warning was online. The instability was not an obvious risk under ss 13–15 CLA, because the flooring only shifted when stepped on and walking across a festival floor is not an obviously risky activity.

Conclusion

Oliver's damages are likely to be reduced for contributory negligence, but Skyline has no complete defence.

5. Remedies

Law

The typical remedy in tort is compensatory damages. These aim to restore the plaintiff, so far as money can, to the position he would have been in had the tort not occurred, and they are assessed once and for all, so future losses must be estimated.

Application

Oliver may claim ordinary damages for:

  • medical expenses: $36,000;
  • rehabilitation program: $5,000;
  • lost income: six months of a $95,000 annual salary = $95,000 ÷ 12 × 6 = $47,500.

These total $88,500. Oliver may also claim an amount for pain and suffering from a serious back injury and extended hospitalisation and, because damages are assessed once and for all, any future medical costs or loss of earning capacity supported by medical evidence (the facts do not indicate any, so this is noted as an assumption). Applying a 25% reduction for contributory negligence, Oliver would recover 75% of $88,500 = $66,375, plus 75% of any general damages awarded.

Overall conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, Oliver can bring an action against Skyline in the tort of negligence and is likely to recover damages for his medical expenses, lost income and rehabilitation costs (approximately $66,375 after a reduction for contributory negligence).

B. 2025 Semester 2, Q1: Liam v Gladstone Recreation Authority (25 marks)

Question text (click)

Located in the city of Gladstone is a public swimming facility known as the Oceanside Leisure Centre. This facility includes an outdoor artificial lake, which is used for water activities and maintained by the Gladstone Recreation Authority (GRA).

In June 2024, during routine maintenance, GRA staff discovered that an underground drainage pipe, located in a deeper part of the lake, had become partially exposed during a storm. The exposed pipe created a strong current, posing a significant risk to swimmers. Internal reports recommended either closing the lake for a period of time while the problem was fixed or placing warning signs to alert swimmers of the potential danger. However, due to an upcoming summer festival that relied on the lake for activities, GRA officials decided not to take immediate action, opting instead to assess the issue after the festival.

During the festival, Liam, a 25-year-old competitive swimmer, entered the lake in a designated swimming zone and swam into the deeper water. Unaware of the exposed pipe, he was suddenly pulled under the water by the strong current. At the time of the incident, signs prohibiting swimming outside of designated areas were posted around the lake. Witnesses reported that Liam had been warned by GRA staff not to swim out too far from the shoreline, but he ignored these warnings and swam into the deeper section of the lake.

Though lifeguards were able to rescue him, Liam suffered severe lung damage due to water inhalation and required prolonged hospitalisation. The hospital fees totalled $189,000. Furthermore, Liam now experiences chronic respiratory issues, affecting his ability to train and compete in swimming events that previously earned him $60,000 each year. Liam was unable to find suitable work for 12 months after his release from hospital. Finally, Liam became addicted to the prescription medication that he was given in hospital. He sought treatment through a drug rehabilitation program, which cost $8,000.

Liam comes to you seeking advice. He wishes to make a claim for damages under Tort Law. Advise Liam on all possible arguments. Please use ILAC to answer this question.

Answer plan
  1. Duty: public authority as occupier of an artificial lake. Hidden risk (current from exposed pipe) → Nagle, Swain; distinguish Romeo, Vairy (obvious, natural). GRA: deep water is obvious + signs.
  2. Breach: s 9(1)(a) internal reports = actual knowledge; (b) "significant risk"; (c) reports themselves recommended closure or signs. s 9(2): probability (festival crowds), seriousness (drowning), burden (signs cheap), social utility (festival ≠ Watt). Distinguish Bolton.
  3. Causation per head: (a) $189k hospital: but-for (would he have heeded a sign?) + scope. (b) lost earnings, 12 months × $60k + future capacity: other causes? (Schneider resignation point). (c) $8k rehab: prescription → distinguish Yates, Schneider.
  4. Defences: CN strong (ignored staff warning + signs; Ingram). VAR/obvious risk: fails, as he was unaware of the pipe (Agar v Hyde); ss 17–19 dangerous recreational activity arguable but weak.
  5. Remedies: $189,000 + $60,000 + $8,000 = $257,000 + future loss of earning capacity + pain and suffering; −30% → $179,900. Exemplary? (deliberate decision). Conclusion.

Whether Liam can recover damages from the Gladstone Recreation Authority ('GRA') depends on whether GRA, as a public authority, owed him a duty of care, whether it breached that duty, whether the breach caused each of his losses, and whether any defences apply.

Issue

Can Liam bring an action against GRA for the tort of negligence?

Law: Negligence generally

A person commits the tort of negligence if they carelessly cause harm to another. Liam must establish on the balance of probabilities that GRA (1) owed him a duty of care, (2) breached that duty, and (3) caused his loss through that breach, with the loss falling within the scope of GRA's liability. Defences and remedies must then be considered under the common law and the Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld) ('CLA').

1. Duty of care

Law

Under the neighbour principle in Donoghue v Stevenson [1932] AC 562, a person must take reasonable care to avoid acts or omissions that could reasonably foreseeably injure persons closely and directly affected by them. An occupier owes a duty to persons entering its premises to ensure they are safe: Australian Safeway Stores Pty Ltd v Zaluzna (1987). Public authorities often control public spaces such as waterways and may be occupiers. However, it is questionable whether they automatically owe a duty of care. This depends on whether there is a hidden risk requiring a warning or prevention, or whether the risk is so obvious to a reasonable person that no duty exists. In Nagle v Rottnest Island Authority (1993), a submerged rock in an area signposted as a 'Swimming area' was a hidden risk, and because the sign encouraged swimming there a duty to warn was owed. In Swain v Waverley (2005), a submerged sandbar between the flags at a patrolled beach was a hidden risk giving rise to a duty to warn. By contrast, in Romeo v Conservation Commission (1998) (an unfenced cliff top) and Vairy v Wyong Shire Council (2005) (diving from a natural rock platform at an unpatrolled beach) the risks were obvious and no liability arose.

Application

GRA is a public authority that maintains and controls the artificial lake at the Oceanside Leisure Centre, so it is the occupier of the lake. Similar to Nagle, GRA marked out a 'designated swimming zone' and was running a festival that relied on the lake, which actively encouraged people to swim there. Like the submerged rock in Nagle and the sandbar in Swain, the exposed pipe was underwater and invisible, and the strong current it created was a hidden risk that Liam was 'unaware' of.

GRA will argue, relying on Romeo and Vairy, that the risk of swimming into deep water away from the shore is obvious, particularly to a competitive swimmer, and that signs prohibited swimming outside designated areas. However, those cases concerned natural features, such as cliffs and rock platforms, whose dangers anyone could see. Here, the lake is artificial, the hazard was created by GRA's own drainage infrastructure, and the danger that materialised was not deep water itself but a concealed current. A reasonable person would not have known about it.

Conclusion

GRA owed Liam a duty of care to warn of, or prevent, the hidden risk created by the exposed pipe.

2. Breach of duty

Law

Under s 9(1) CLA, a person breaches their duty by failing to take precautions against a risk of harm if (a) the risk was foreseeable, being one they knew or ought reasonably to have known about, (b) it was not insignificant, and (c) a reasonable person in their position would have taken those precautions. Under s 9(2), the court considers the probability of harm, the likely seriousness of the harm (Paris v Stepney Borough Council (1951)), the burden of taking precautions, and the social utility of the activity (Watt v Hertfordshire County Council (1954), where the urgency of saving a life justified the risk taken). In Bolton v Stone (1951), a cricket club was not in breach when a ball struck a person outside the ground, because such hits were extremely rare and the burden of eliminating that risk was high.

Application

Foreseeability (s 9(1)(a)). GRA actually knew of the risk. Its own staff discovered the exposed pipe during routine maintenance, and internal reports identified the danger.

Not insignificant (s 9(1)(b)). GRA's own reports described the current as 'posing a significant risk to swimmers', so the risk was clearly not insignificant.

Reasonable person (s 9(1)(c) and s 9(2)). A reasonable authority would have either closed the lake or posted specific warning signs, which are exactly the precautions its internal reports recommended.

  • Probability (a): The probability of harm was high. A festival would bring large numbers of swimmers into the lake, and it was foreseeable that some, especially strong swimmers, would move into the deeper water where the pipe was located.
  • Seriousness (b): Being pulled under by a strong current can cause drowning, death or, as here, severe lung damage. As in Paris v Stepney, where the seriousness of the consequences required precautions, the gravity of the potential harm here was extreme.
  • Burden (c): GRA will argue that closing the lake during the festival would have been costly and disruptive. However, the alternative precaution, warning signs about the current or roping off the deeper section, was cheap and quick and would not have stopped the festival.
  • Social utility (d): The festival had community and recreational value. However, unlike the emergency rescue in Watt v Hertfordshire, it was not urgent, and its commercial and recreational purposes do not justify exposing swimmers to a known and significant danger.

GRA may point to the general signs prohibiting swimming outside designated areas and the staff warnings not to swim too far out. However, these were generic and did not tell swimmers about the specific danger of the current. This case is clearly distinguishable from Bolton v Stone. There, the risk was remote and costly to eliminate. Here, the risk was known, documented as significant and likely to eventuate during a festival, and simple precautions were recommended but deliberately postponed.

Conclusion

GRA breached its duty of care to Liam under s 9 CLA.

3. Causation

Law

Under s 11(1) CLA, Liam must show (a) factual causation, meaning the breach was a necessary condition of the harm, tested by asking whether 'but for' the breach the harm would have occurred, and (b) that it is appropriate for GRA's scope of liability to extend to the harm, meaning the type of harm was reasonably foreseeable and not too remote: Overseas Tankships v Morts [1961]. In Yates v Jones [1990], an addiction to heroin suggested by a friend while the plaintiff was in hospital after a car accident was held to have been caused by the friend, not the accident. In Schneider v Smith & Anor (2016), the plaintiff's deliberate use of illegal drugs, outside medical advice, to cope with pain was not caused by the accident. However, medical and psychological treatment costs were recoverable as 'ordinary and natural consequences', and losses from his resignation were not recoverable because there were other causes unrelated to the accident.

Application

(a) Lung damage and hospital fees ($189,000). Factual causation: But for GRA's failure to close the lake or warn of the current, Liam would not have been pulled under. GRA will argue that Liam ignored staff warnings and signs, so he would have ignored any further warning as well. However, the warnings he received were general ('don't swim too far'). A specific warning of a dangerous current from an exposed pipe, or closure of the lake, would likely have prevented a competitive swimmer from entering that area. Closure would certainly have done so. Scope of liability: Severe lung damage from water inhalation is precisely the type of harm that is foreseeable from a strong underwater current, and the hospital fees are its ordinary and natural consequence. Both tests are satisfied.

(b) Lost earnings and chronic respiratory issues. Factual causation: But for the incident, Liam would not have developed chronic respiratory issues and would have continued to earn around $60,000 a year from swimming. Scope of liability: Loss of income while recovering, and reduced capacity to compete, are foreseeable consequences of serious lung damage to an athlete. GRA may argue, relying on Schneider, where lost income from the plaintiff's resignation was refused because of other causes, that Liam's inability to find 'suitable work' for 12 months reflects other factors, such as the job market or his own choices. No such external causes appear on the facts. It is assumed the 12 months of lost income resulted from his injury, so this loss is likely within scope.

(c) Addiction and rehabilitation ($8,000). Factual causation: But for the injury, Liam would not have been given the prescription medication in hospital or become addicted. Scope of liability: This case is distinguishable from Yates v Jones, where the addictive drug (heroin) was suggested by a friend, and from Schneider, where the plaintiff deliberately chose illegal drugs outside medical advice. Here, the medication was prescribed to Liam in hospital as part of his treatment. Assuming he took it as directed (the facts do not suggest otherwise), there was no voluntary, unreasonable act breaking the chain. Consistent with Schneider, the cost of treating a consequence of medical treatment is an ordinary and natural consequence of the injury and is within GRA's scope of liability.

Conclusion

GRA's breach caused Liam's hospital fees, lost earnings and rehabilitation costs, all of which are within the scope of GRA's liability.

4. Defences

Law

Where the plaintiff contributed to their own injury, liability is apportioned and damages reduced: Ingram v Britten [1994]; Manley v Alexander. Voluntary assumption of risk is a complete defence but is very difficult to establish. The plaintiff must have had full knowledge and appreciation of the precise risk that eventuated and freely accepted it: Agar v Hyde (2000). Under ss 13–14 CLA, a plaintiff is presumed aware of an obvious risk unless they prove otherwise; under s 15 there is no duty to warn of an obvious risk; and under ss 17–19 there is no liability for harm from an obvious risk of a dangerous recreational activity.

Application

Contributory negligence: This is GRA's strongest argument. Liam ignored signs prohibiting swimming outside designated areas and ignored direct warnings from GRA staff not to swim far from the shore, and he deliberately swam into deeper water. Similar to the plaintiff in Ingram v Britten, whose own careless conduct contributed to his injury despite the defendant's failure, Liam failed to take reasonable care for his own safety. As a competitive swimmer, he should have appreciated the importance of following safety instructions. Against this, he did start in a designated zone and could not have known of the hidden current. A significant reduction, perhaps around 30%, is likely.

Voluntary assumption of risk / obvious risk: GRA may argue that Liam, an experienced swimmer who ignored warnings, voluntarily assumed the risk of deep-water swimming, or that this was an obvious risk of a dangerous recreational activity under ss 17–19 CLA. However, unlike the rugby players in Agar v Hyde, who knew the precise risks inherent in a scrum, Liam was 'unaware of the exposed pipe'. The precise risk that eventuated, a strong current, was hidden, not obvious, so he cannot be presumed aware of it under ss 13–14. Recreational swimming in a managed lake is also unlikely to be a 'dangerous' recreational activity. This defence is likely to fail.

Conclusion

Liam's damages are likely to be reduced for contributory negligence, but GRA has no complete defence.

5. Remedies

Law

Tort damages are compensatory and aim to restore the plaintiff, so far as money can, to his position had the tort not occurred. Ordinary damages are assessed once and for all, so future losses must be estimated. Exemplary damages may be awarded where the defendant deliberately and maliciously disregarded the plaintiff's rights.

Application

Liam may claim:

  • hospital fees: $189,000;
  • lost income for 12 months after release: 12 months of $60,000 a year = $60,000 (plus income lost during his hospitalisation, which is not quantified in the facts);
  • drug rehabilitation: $8,000.

These total $257,000. Because damages are assessed once and for all, Liam should also claim an estimated amount for future loss of earning capacity, since his chronic respiratory issues affect his ability to train and compete, and an amount for pain and suffering. Applying a 30% reduction for contributory negligence, Liam would recover 70% of $257,000 = $179,900, plus 70% of any future and general damages. Liam may also argue for exemplary damages because GRA consciously chose to put the festival ahead of known safety recommendations. However, as GRA's conduct was not malicious, such an award is unlikely.

Margin note: showing the arithmetic (12 × $60k/12, then the % reduction) is cheap marks. No calculators are allowed, so choose round percentages.

Overall conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, Liam can bring an action against GRA in the tort of negligence and is likely to recover damages for his hospital fees, lost and future earnings and rehabilitation costs, reduced by approximately 30% for his contributory negligence.

Recurring twists across all the papers — and the paragraph that handles each

The examiner reuses the same seven twists. Each card says where it has appeared, then gives the paragraph to copy, with the [facts] left for you to fill in.

① Warnings, complaints and signs

Betty 2024 S1 (5 reports to RMO) · Margaret 2023 S2 (3 reports) · Liam 2025 S2 (internal reports; signs; staff warned Liam) · Oliver 2026 S1 (2 reports; Facebook post only) · Tom 2021 S2 ("Safe water conditions" sign; prior injuries)

(a) Defendant was warned and did nothing → breach, s 9(1)(a)–(b)

The risk was foreseeable under s 9(1)(a) CLA because [D] actually knew of it: [D] received [number] separate reports warning that [hazard] was a hazard. [D] dismissed them because [no accidents had happened before], but the risk was not insignificant under s 9(1)(b): the reports showed it was real, and the possible harm was serious personal injury. [D] may rely on Bolton v Stone, where the cricket club was not liable because a ball had only rarely left the ground in many years. That case can be distinguished: here, [number] warnings were received within [time], which shows the risk was likely, not remote.

In context: Answer 1 breach · Answer 2 breach

(b) Defendant's sign said "safe" / "swim here" → hidden risk, duty to warn

Similar to Nagle v Rottnest Island Authority, where a "swimming area" sign encouraged people to swim over a submerged rock, [D]'s sign stating "[wording]" encouraged [P] to [swim / dive / walk] there. The [sandbar / pipe / rock] could not be seen, so it was a hidden risk, not an obvious one. Unlike Vairy v Wyong Shire Council, where the beach was unpatrolled and had no signs, [D] invited the public to use this area, so it owed a duty to warn of or remove the hidden risk.

In context: Answer 5 duty

(c) Warning only online / ineffective (Oliver: Facebook post)

Under s 9(2)(c) CLA the burden of taking proper precautions was low: [D] could have placed a physical warning sign or barrier at [site]. A warning posted only on [Facebook] was not a precaution a reasonable person would think enough, because [patrons] walking past the hazard would not be reading it at the time.

(d) Plaintiff ignored warnings / signs → contributory negligence (NOT voluntary assumption of risk unless P knew the precise risk)

[D] may argue that [P] voluntarily assumed the risk. However, this defence is very difficult to establish: [P] must have had full knowledge of the precise risk that happened and freely accepted it. Unlike the players in Agar v Hyde, who knew the risks inherent in rugby, [P] did not know about [the hidden hazard]; he was warned only about [swimming too far out]. The stronger defence is contributory negligence: by ignoring [the staff warnings and signs], [P] failed to take reasonable care for his own safety, so his damages are likely to be reduced (Ingram v Britten).

In context: Answer 5 defences

② Intoxication — whose? It changes which element it goes to

Logan the drink-driver (Betty 2024 S1) · drunk surgeon Dr Phillips (Matt 2022 S2) · Oliver drinking at the festival (2026 S1)

Who is drunkGoes toParagraph
A third party who also caused the accident (driver)Causation — material contribution: D is still liablesee ④ below; Answer 1 hospital bill
A later third party who makes things worse (surgeon)Causation — break in the chain (Yates v Jones); sue the doctor tooAnswer 3 surgeon
The plaintiffDefence — contributory negligence (Manley v Alexander)below
[D] will argue contributory negligence because [P] had consumed [several alcoholic drinks] and was [not looking where he was walking]. Similar to Manley v Alexander, where the plaintiff drank 12 stubbies and fell asleep in the middle of the road before the defendant negligently drove over him, [P]'s own carelessness contributed to his injury. Liability is therefore likely to be apportioned between [D] and [P], reducing his damages, although [D] remains liable for its own share.

③ Plaintiff distracted (phone)

Betty on footpath transferring money (2024 S1) · Matt holding phone while driving (2022 S2) · MCQ "Jenny on her phone, 70/30" (Exemplars)

The defence of contributory negligence is relevant because [P] was [using her phone] and not paying attention. If [P] contributed to her own injury, liability is apportioned between the parties (Ingram v Britten). On one hand, [D] will argue that an attentive [pedestrian / driver] may have [seen the car / reacted in time]. On the other hand, [P] will argue that [the car was out of control and hit her suddenly], so that even with full attention she could not have avoided it. Unlike Manley v Alexander, [P] was [where she was entitled to be]. Any reduction is therefore likely to be [small / significant].

In context: Answer 1 defences · Answer 3 defences

④ Third-party causes / several causes of the same harm

Drunk driver + pothole (Betty) · SoundCo speaker burst + unstable flooring (Oliver 2026 S1) · job pressure + lost sale (Tom 2022 S1) · crash injuries + surgeon's errors → year off work (Matt)

The "but for" test is not suitable where the harm has several causes. In that case the court applies the material contribution test: [D] is liable if its carelessness increased the risk of injury and that risk happened, even if other factors also contributed (Chappel v Hart, McHugh J). It is not necessary for [D]'s breach to be the only cause. Similar to Cook v ACT Racing Club, where an overheated sauna was only one cause of a jockey's heart attack but still significantly increased the risk, [D]'s failure to [repair the pothole / fix the flooring] materially contributed to [P]'s injury, even though [the drunk driver / SoundCo's malfunction] also contributed.
Who to sue: name every possible defendant in one line — Betty: RMO and Logan; Matt: Harry, and separately Dr Phillips / the hospital (employer vicariously liable for acts in the course of employment, Deatons v Flew); Oliver: Skyline, and possibly SoundCo. Then concentrate on the defendant the question names.

In context: Answer 1 · Answer 3 wages · Answer 4 depression

⑤ Addiction — the three versions (appears in 5 of the 7 tort papers)

VersionPaperResultCase
Taken exactly as prescribed, became addictedOliver 2026 S1; Liam 2025 S2; Margaret 2023 S2 (first stage)Recoverable — part of the prescribed treatmentDistinguish Schneider & Yates
P exceeded the dose / continued on own without the doctor knowingMatt 2022 S2; Margaret (exceeded "as a result of the addiction")Matt: not recoverable; Margaret: arguable, probably recoverableSchneider v Smith
Someone else suggested it (friend / husband)Betty 2024 S1Not recoverable — chain brokenYates v Jones

Prescribed version (recoverable)

[D] will argue that the addiction was not caused by its breach. However, but for the accident, [P] would not have been prescribed [the medication], and he took it in accordance with medical advice. This can be distinguished from Schneider v Smith, where the plaintiff's decision to take illegal drugs was a deliberate, voluntary act that was "not part of prescribed or recommended treatment", and from Yates v Jones, where the addiction was caused by a friend suggesting heroin. Here, addiction is a natural and foreseeable consequence of the prescribed treatment of the injury, so both tests in s 11 are satisfied and the $[cost] rehabilitation is recoverable.

Exceeded on own (not recoverable)

But for the accident, [P] would not have been prescribed the medication. However, [P] chose to continue taking it for [a further 3 months] without his doctor knowing. Similar to Schneider v Smith, this was a deliberate, voluntary act that was not part of prescribed or recommended treatment, and the law of torts is built on individual responsibility. The addiction is therefore outside the scope of [D]'s liability under s 11(1)(b), and the $[cost] is not recoverable.

Third person suggested it (not recoverable)

Similar to Yates v Jones, where the plaintiff's heroin addiction was held to be caused by the friend who suggested it, not by the car accident, [P]'s addiction was caused by [her husband]'s suggestion that she take more than the prescribed dose. This breaks the chain of causation, so the $[cost] is not recoverable from [D].

In context: Answer 1 · Answer 2 · Answer 3

⑥ Psychiatric harm (PTSD, depression, anxiety)

Betty PTSD · Matt PTSD · Tom depression + job pressure (2022 S1) · Bill (child witness, 2021 S2)

Nervous shock, or mental and psychiatric injury, is a recognised type of injury in negligence; indeed Mrs Donoghue herself suffered nervous shock in Donoghue v Stevenson. But for the accident, [P] would not have developed [PTSD]: the [nightmares and flashbacks] began after it and no other cause is suggested. Similar to Schneider v Smith, where damages for psychological treatment were awarded because they were "ordinary and natural consequences" of the accident, [P]'s therapy costs of $[cost] are reasonably foreseeable and are recoverable.
The course slides do not teach special rules for mental harm (no "secondary victim" cases). Handle a witness like Bill through the neighbour principle + s 11 scope of liability, and say you are doing so. Do not bring in cases from outside the course.

In context: Answer 1 · Answer 4 (other causes) · Answer 5 (Bill)

⑧ Remedies paragraph (every answer ends with this)

The usual remedy in tort is ordinary damages, which aim to place [P], so far as money can, in the position she would have been in had the tort not been committed. Damages are assessed "once and for all", so [P] must claim any future losses now. [P] can therefore claim [$X + $Y = $total], reduced by any contributory negligence. Exemplary damages are unlikely because [D]'s conduct was careless rather than a deliberate and malicious disregard of [P]'s rights.

Answer 1 — 2024 S1 Q2: Betty (pothole, drunk driver, PTSD, painkiller addiction)

Question text — Semester One 2024, Question 2 (25 marks)

Woobalong is a residential suburb in the city of Rockhampton, which is located in Central Queensland. Over the last 5 years, parts of Central Queensland have been subjected to extensive flooding due to heavier than normal rainfall. As a result of constant localized flooding in the Rockhampton area, one section of the main road (Sefton Road) in Woobalong has suffered significant deterioration which has led to the formation of a deep pothole in the middle of the road. (A pothole is a depression or hollow in a road surface caused by wear and tear and/or adverse weather.) Furthermore, the angle and position of the pothole makes it impossible for road users to detect.

In January 2024, the Rockhampton Mayor's office (RMO) received five separate reports from Woobalong residents warning that the pothole was bad in a bad position and was a hazard. However, the complaints were dismissed by the RMO on the basis that there had been no reported road incidents or car accidents at that location on Sefton Road in the past.

One afternoon in February 2024, Betty is walking on the footpath along Sefton Road, on her way home from work. She is looking at her phone, transferring money onto her credit card and not paying much attention to her surroundings. Suddenly, Betty is hit by an out-of-control car, driven by Logan. An investigation finds that Logan lost control of the car when his tyres hit the pothole. The police also found that Logan was over the legal blood/breath alcohol limit at the time of the incident.

As a result of the accident, Betty is hospitalized with extensive injuries. After 6 months in intensive care and one year in rehabilitation, her total hospital bill amounts to $755,000. During her stay in hospital, Betty's husband suggests that she takes a higher dosage of painkillers than what is prescribed by her doctor to reduce the severe pain she is experiencing. Betty develops an addiction to these painkillers and later checks herself in to a rehabilitation clinic to help treat her addiction, at a cost of $20,000. After the accident, Betty experiences nightmares, flash backs and sleep deprivation. She is diagnosed with severe PTSD (post-traumatic stress disorder) and has to undergo weekly therapy for four years, at a cost of $79,000.

Betty comes to you seeking advice and asks whether she can recover the amounts she paid for the hospital bill, rehabilitation clinic and therapy.

Advise Betty as to her most appropriate course of action under the Tort Law. Please use ILAC to answer this question.

Answer plan (6 lines)
  1. Issue: Can Betty bring an action against the RMO for the tort of negligence? (Logan = second defendant, short.)
  2. Duty: Donoghue neighbour principle; public authority controls road; hidden risk → Nagle, Swain (contrast Romeo, Vairy).
  3. Breach: s 9(1)(a)–(c) — five reports; "no accidents" = Bolton v Stone argument, distinguish; s 9(2): seriousness (Paris), cheap burden, Watt.
  4. Causation, each head: hospital $755k ✔ (material contribution despite Logan — Chappel, Cook); rehab $20k ✘ (husband → Yates; Schneider); PTSD $79k ✔ (Schneider).
  5. Defences: contributory negligence (phone) — weak, Manley distinguished; voluntary assumption of risk not relevant.
  6. Remedies: ordinary damages $834,000 less any reduction; also sue Logan.

Whether Betty can recover her hospital bill ($755,000), rehabilitation clinic fees ($20,000) and therapy costs ($79,000) depends on whether she can establish the tort of negligence against the Rockhampton Mayor's Office (RMO), and then whether each of these losses was caused by the RMO's breach. Her most appropriate course of action is an action in negligence against the RMO; she may also join Logan as a defendant (see Issue 2).

Issue 1

ISSUECan Betty bring an action against the RMO for the tort of negligence?

Law

A person commits the tort of negligence if they carelessly cause harm to another person. To succeed, Betty must prove on the balance of probabilities that: (1) the RMO owed her a duty of care; (2) the RMO breached that duty; and (3) the breach caused her loss (causation). The court will then consider any defences and the remedy. In Queensland the relevant statute is the Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld) (CLA).

Duty of care

In Donoghue v Stevenson [1932] AC 562, Mrs Donoghue suffered nervous shock and gastro-enteritis after drinking ginger beer from an opaque bottle containing a decomposed snail. The House of Lords held that the manufacturer owed her a duty of care. Lord Atkin's "neighbour principle" states that you must take reasonable care to avoid acts or omissions which you can reasonably foresee would be likely to injure your neighbour, being persons so closely and directly affected by your act that you ought reasonably to have them in contemplation. The question is whether it was reasonably foreseeable that the defendant's conduct could harm someone in the plaintiff's position.

A duty is easily found where the relationship falls into an established category, such as occupiers, who owe a duty to people who come onto their premises (Australian Safeway Stores Pty Ltd v Zaluzna (1987)). Public authorities such as councils have control over public spaces like roads, parks and waterways and can be occupiers. Whether they owe a duty depends on whether there is a hidden risk requiring a warning or prevention, or whether the risk is so obvious to a reasonable person that no duty exists. In Nagle v Rottnest Island Authority (1993), a submerged rock in an area signed as a "swimming area" was a hidden risk, and the authority owed a duty. In Swain v Waverley (2005), a submerged sandbar at a patrolled beach was a hidden risk and there was a duty to warn. By contrast, in Romeo v Conservation Commission (1998) (unfenced cliff top) and Vairy v Wyong Shire Council (2005) (natural rock platform at an unpatrolled beach with no signs), the risks were obvious and the authorities were not liable.

Breach of duty

Under s 9(1) CLA, a defendant breaches its duty by failing to take precautions against a risk of harm if: (a) the risk was foreseeable (one the defendant knew or ought reasonably to have known about); (b) the risk was not insignificant; and (c) in the circumstances, a reasonable person in the defendant's position would have taken those precautions. Under s 9(2), in deciding what a reasonable person would have done, the court considers: (a) the probability of harm; (b) the likely seriousness of the harm; (c) the burden of taking precautions; and (d) the social utility of the activity. In Bolton v Stone (1951), a cricket ball hit out of the ground struck a passer-by; because balls had only very rarely cleared the ground, the probability of harm was so low that the club was not liable. In Paris v Stepney Borough Council (1951), a welder with sight in only one eye was not given goggles and lost the sight of his other eye; the seriousness of the harm meant precautions should have been taken. In Watt v Hertfordshire (1954), a fireman injured by unsecured equipment on the way to an emergency failed, because the social utility of saving life justified the risk.

Causation

Section 11(1) CLA sets out two tests, which must be applied to each loss. (a) Factual causation: the breach must be a necessary condition of the harm — "but for" the defendant's carelessness, would the plaintiff have suffered the harm? Where the harm has several causes, the court applies the material contribution test: the defendant is liable if its conduct increased the risk of injury and that risk happened, even if other factors also contributed (Chappel v Hart (1998), McHugh J). In Cook v ACT Racing Club Inc [2001], an overheated sauna was only one cause of a jockey's heart attack, but it significantly increased the risk, so the club was liable. (b) Scope of liability: it must be appropriate for liability to extend to the harm, which requires the type of harm to be reasonably foreseeable and not too remote (Overseas Tankships v Morts [1961]). In Yates v Jones [1990], a car accident victim became addicted to heroin after a friend suggested it to ease her pain; the addiction was held to be caused by the friend, not the accident. In Schneider v Smith (2016), the plaintiff's use of illegal drugs to cope with pain was a deliberate, voluntary act that was not part of prescribed or recommended treatment, so causation failed for it; but damages for medical costs and psychological treatment were awarded as ordinary and natural consequences of the accident.

Defences

If the plaintiff contributed to her own injury, liability is apportioned between the parties (contributory negligence): Ingram v Britten (plaintiff driving a tractor at speed, defendant had not secured its frame); Manley v Alexander (plaintiff drank 12 stubbies and slept in the middle of the road; defendant negligently drove over him).

Application

Duty of care

The RMO is a public authority with control over Sefton Road, a main road in Woobalong. Assumption: the facts do not say who maintains the road, so I assume the RMO (on behalf of the council) is responsible for it. The pothole's angle and position made it "impossible for road users to detect". Similar to the submerged rock in Nagle and the sandbar in Swain, this is a hidden risk, not an obvious one like the cliff edge in Romeo or the rock platform in Vairy. The RMO may argue that the pothole endangers drivers, not pedestrians on the footpath. However, applying the neighbour principle in Donoghue, it is reasonably foreseeable that a car losing control on a deep pothole on a main road will leave the road and strike people walking on the adjoining footpath. Betty is therefore a person closely and directly affected, and the RMO owed her a duty of care.

Breach of duty

s 9(1)(a) — foreseeable: The risk was not only foreseeable but actually known. In January 2024 the RMO received five separate reports warning that the pothole was in a bad position and was a hazard.

s 9(1)(b) — not insignificant: The RMO dismissed the complaints because there had been no reported incidents. However, a deep, undetectable pothole in the middle of a main road, getting worse through five years of flooding, is a real risk, as five separate residents recognised.

s 9(1)(c) and s 9(2) — reasonable person: As to probability, the RMO will rely on Bolton v Stone, arguing that, with no history of accidents, the chance of harm was remote. This can be distinguished: in Bolton the low risk was proven by many years of play, whereas here the hazard is recent and growing, it is on a main road used every day, and five warnings arrived within one month. As to seriousness, a car losing control can cause death or catastrophic injury; like the loss of sight in Paris, such serious possible harm demands precautions. As to the burden, warning signs, a lower speed limit, a barrier or filling the pothole would have been relatively quick and inexpensive. The RMO may argue that flood damage across the region stretches its resources, but no facts suggest it could not afford to deal with one reported hazard. As to social utility, maintaining roads is useful, but unlike the emergency in Watt, nothing justified leaving the hazard in place. A reasonable authority would have taken precautions, so the RMO breached its duty.

Causation — applied to each loss

(i) Hospital bill — $755,000. Factual causation: The investigation found that Logan lost control "when his tyres hit the pothole". The RMO will argue that the real cause was Logan's drink-driving. Because there are several causes, the material contribution test applies. Similar to Cook, where the sauna was only one cause of the heart attack, the pothole need not be the only cause of the accident; it is enough that the RMO's failure to repair it increased the risk of exactly this kind of accident and that risk happened (Chappel v Hart). Scope of liability: Physical injury to a person near the road from an out-of-control car is the very type of harm residents warned of, so it is reasonably foreseeable and not remote (Overseas Tankships). Both tests are satisfied and the $755,000 is recoverable.

(ii) Rehabilitation clinic — $20,000. Factual causation: Betty will argue that, but for the accident, she would not have been in severe pain or taking painkillers at all. However, her addiction arose because her husband suggested she take a higher dose than her doctor prescribed. Similar to Yates v Jones, where the friend's suggestion of heroin, not the car accident, was held to cause the addiction, the husband's suggestion is an intervening act that breaks the chain of causation. Scope of liability: As in Schneider, taking medication beyond the prescription was a voluntary act that was not part of prescribed or recommended treatment, and the law expects individual responsibility. Betty may argue she was in severe pain and vulnerable in hospital, but on balance the addiction is outside the scope of the RMO's liability, and the $20,000 is unlikely to be recoverable.

(iii) PTSD therapy — $79,000. Factual causation: Betty's nightmares, flashbacks and sleep deprivation began after the accident and no other cause is suggested, so but for the accident she would not have developed PTSD. Scope of liability: Psychiatric injury (nervous shock) is a recognised type of injury, as in Donoghue itself. Similar to Schneider, where psychological treatment was compensated as an ordinary and natural consequence of the accident, PTSD after being struck by a car is reasonably foreseeable. The RMO may dispute whether four years of weekly therapy is reasonable, but that goes to the amount, not liability. The $79,000 is recoverable.

Defences

The RMO will argue contributory negligence because Betty was looking at her phone, transferring money and "not paying much attention to her surroundings". However, unlike the plaintiff in Manley v Alexander, who lay drunk in the middle of the road, Betty was walking on the footpath where pedestrians are entitled to be, and the car was "out-of-control" and hit her "suddenly". Even an attentive pedestrian is unlikely to have avoided it. Any reduction for contributory negligence is therefore likely to be small, if any. Voluntary assumption of risk is not relevant, as Betty had no knowledge of the pothole and did not accept any risk.

Feedback: only discuss defences that the facts raise. One line dismissing voluntary assumption of risk is enough.

Conclusion

CONCLUSIONOn the balance of probabilities, Betty can bring an action in negligence against the RMO and recover her hospital bill and PTSD therapy costs, but probably not the rehabilitation clinic fees.

Issue 2 — Logan (alternative defendant)

Short on time? This Logan issue is optional (~250 words). The core answer without it is ~1,900 words; cut the Romeo/Vairy contrast and Watt first if you need to save more.

ISSUECan Betty also bring an action against Logan for the tort of negligence?

LAWMotorists owe a duty of care to other road users, an established category; the same s 9 and s 11 tests apply.

APPLICATIONLogan, as a motorist, owed Betty, a pedestrian beside the road, a duty of care. Driving over the legal alcohol limit creates a foreseeable and not insignificant risk; under s 9(2) the probability and seriousness of harm are high and the burden of not drinking is nil, so a reasonable driver would not have driven. On causation, Logan will argue that the pothole was undetectable and that a sober driver would also have lost control. The facts do not say whether his intoxication affected his control, so I assume that it at least reduced his ability to react; on that basis it materially contributed to the accident (Chappel v Hart). The same analysis of each loss applies as above.

CONCLUSIONBetty should sue both the RMO and Logan, so that she can recover even if one of them is found not liable.

Remedies

The usual remedy is ordinary damages, which aim to restore Betty, so far as money can, to the position she would have been in had the tort not occurred. They are assessed "once and for all", so she should also claim any future losses now, such as lost wages (the facts are silent on whether she lost income). Betty can claim $755,000 + $79,000 = $834,000, reduced by any contributory negligence. Exemplary damages are unlikely, as the RMO was careless rather than deliberately and maliciously disregarding her rights.

Answer 2 — 2023 S2 Q2: Margaret (pothole, careful driver, prescribed opioids)

Question text — Semester Two 2023, Question 2 (25 marks)

Sherwood is a suburb in Brisbane managed and maintained by the Brisbane City Council (BCC). Due to wear and tear and environmental conditions over several years, Freshman Street in Sherwood begins to deteriorate, eventually creating a pothole (a pothole is a depression or hollow in a road surface caused by wear or substance). The angle and positioning of the pothole makes it not easily visible to road users.

In 2023, the BCC received three separate reports from Sherwood residents warning that the pothole was a driving risk, with the potential to cause serious road accidents. However, the BCC dismissed these complaints, believing that the chance of injury was insignificant because no car accidents had ever occurred on or near that street.

One morning, Margaret is walking down Freshman Street, on her way to the bus stop. She is hit by an out-of-control car and is hospitalised. An investigation finds that the car lost control due to the driver running over the pothole. It was also determined that the driver was not speeding or driving recklessly at the time.

As a result of the accident, Margaret pays $15,000 in medical fees to treat her broken arm and dislocated shoulder. Margaret also undergoes 3 years' worth of ongoing treatment for severe back pain that costs $65,000. As a result of the back pain, Margaret's doctor prescribes her opioids, which is a class of drug medication used to reduce severe pain. Over time, however, Margaret develops an addiction to these opioids and, as a result of the addiction, gradually starts ingesting them beyond the prescribed dose. She then pays $10,500 to a rehabilitation clinic to treat her addiction.

Advise Margaret as to the most appropriate action she can bring under Tort Law and as to any damages (if any) she is able to claim. Please use ILAC to answer this question.

Answer plan (6 lines)
  1. Issue: Can Margaret bring an action against the BCC for the tort of negligence? (Driver not negligent — one line.)
  2. Duty: neighbour principle; BCC "manages and maintains" street; pothole not easily visible = hidden risk (Nagle, Swain v Romeo, Vairy).
  3. Breach: three reports; BCC said "insignificant" → rebut s 9(1)(b); Bolton distinguished; Paris; low burden.
  4. Causation: $15k ✔; $65k ✔; $10.5k rehab — prescribed opioids, distinguish Yates & Schneider → probably ✔.
  5. Defences: no facts of contributory negligence (state assumption); VAR irrelevant.
  6. Remedies: ordinary damages $90,500.

Margaret's most appropriate action is in the tort of negligence against the Brisbane City Council (BCC). Whether she can recover her medical fees ($15,000), back treatment ($65,000) and rehabilitation costs ($10,500) depends on whether she can establish duty, breach and causation for each loss.

The driver was "not speeding or driving recklessly", so there is no breach by the driver — say so in one sentence and focus on the BCC. That is what "most appropriate action" is testing.

Issue

ISSUECan Margaret bring an action against the BCC for the tort of negligence?

Law

A person commits the tort of negligence if they carelessly cause harm to another. Margaret must prove on the balance of probabilities that (1) the BCC owed her a duty of care; (2) the BCC breached that duty; and (3) the breach caused her loss; any defences and remedies are then considered. The Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld) (CLA) applies.

Duty of care

Under Lord Atkin's neighbour principle in Donoghue v Stevenson [1932] AC 562, a person must take reasonable care to avoid acts or omissions which they can reasonably foresee would be likely to injure persons so closely and directly affected that they ought to have them in contemplation. In that case, a manufacturer owed a duty to the consumer of a ginger beer containing a decomposed snail which could not be seen through the bottle. Occupiers owe a duty to people who come onto their premises (Australian Safeway Stores Pty Ltd v Zaluzna (1987)). Public authorities that control public spaces such as roads can be occupiers, and they will owe a duty where there is a hidden risk that requires a warning or prevention, rather than a risk that is obvious to a reasonable person. In Nagle v Rottnest Island Authority (1993) a submerged rock in a signed swimming area, and in Swain v Waverley (2005) a submerged sandbar at a patrolled beach, were hidden risks giving rise to a duty. In Romeo v Conservation Commission (1998) and Vairy v Wyong Shire Council (2005), the risks (a cliff edge; diving from a natural rock platform) were obvious and the authorities were not liable.

Breach of duty

Under s 9(1) CLA, the BCC breached its duty if (a) the risk was foreseeable, (b) the risk was not insignificant, and (c) a reasonable person in its position would have taken precautions. Under s 9(2), the court considers (a) the probability of harm, (b) its likely seriousness, (c) the burden of taking precautions, and (d) the social utility of the activity. In Bolton v Stone (1951) a cricket club was not liable when a ball left the ground and hit a passer-by, because this had happened so rarely that the probability of harm was very low. In Paris v Stepney Borough Council (1951), the serious consequences for a one-eyed welder meant goggles should have been provided.

Causation

Under s 11(1) CLA, (a) the breach must be a necessary condition of the harm ("but for" test — factual causation), and (b) it must be appropriate for the defendant's liability to extend to the harm (scope of liability): the type of harm must be reasonably foreseeable and not too remote (Overseas Tankships v Morts [1961]). Both tests must be applied to each loss. In Yates v Jones [1990], a heroin addiction suggested by a friend was caused by the friend, not the car accident. In Schneider v Smith (2016), illegal drugs taken to cope with pain were a deliberate, voluntary act that was not part of prescribed or recommended treatment, so causation failed; but medical and psychological treatment costs were recoverable as ordinary and natural consequences of the accident.

Defences

If the plaintiff contributed to her own harm, liability is apportioned (contributory negligence): Ingram v Britten; Manley v Alexander.

Application

Duty of care

The BCC "manages and maintains" Sherwood, including Freshman Street, so as the public authority in control of the road it is in the position of an occupier. The pothole's angle and position made it "not easily visible to road users". Similar to the submerged rock in Nagle and the sandbar in Swain, it was a hidden risk, unlike the obvious cliff in Romeo. Applying Donoghue, it is reasonably foreseeable that a car hitting a hidden pothole could lose control and strike a pedestrian walking along that street, so Margaret was the BCC's neighbour and it owed her a duty of care.

Breach of duty

s 9(1)(a): The risk was foreseeable because the BCC actually knew of it: three separate residents reported that the pothole was a driving risk "with the potential to cause serious road accidents".

s 9(1)(b): The BCC believed the chance of injury was "insignificant" because no accidents had occurred. However, the residents' reports, the hidden nature of the pothole and the worsening of the road over several years show that the risk was real and therefore not insignificant.

s 9(1)(c) and s 9(2): On probability, the BCC will rely on Bolton v Stone, arguing that the absence of past accidents shows the chance of harm was low. That case can be distinguished: in Bolton the risk had been tested over many years of matches, whereas this pothole developed recently and three residents independently warned of it within one year. On seriousness, residents specifically warned of "serious road accidents"; like Paris, the gravity of possible harm called for precautions. On burden, repairing the pothole, or at least placing warning signs or cones, was relatively quick and inexpensive. On social utility, maintaining roads is useful, but there was no reason to leave a known hazard in place. A reasonable council would have taken precautions, so the BCC breached its duty.

Causation — applied to each loss

(i) Medical fees for broken arm and dislocated shoulder — $15,000. Factual: The investigation found the car lost control "due to the driver running over the pothole", and the driver was not speeding or reckless, so there is no other cause. But for the BCC's failure to repair or warn of the pothole, Margaret would not have been hit. Scope: Physical injury to a pedestrian from an out-of-control car is precisely the type of harm residents warned of, so it is reasonably foreseeable (Overseas Tankships). Recoverable.

(ii) Three years' treatment for severe back pain — $65,000. Factual: The back pain is stated to be "a result of the accident"; but for the breach, Margaret would not have needed this treatment. Scope: Similar to Schneider, where medical costs were awarded as ordinary and natural consequences of the accident, ongoing treatment of an injury suffered when hit by a car is reasonably foreseeable. The BCC may question whether three years of treatment was reasonable, but that goes to the amount. Recoverable.

(iii) Rehabilitation clinic for opioid addiction — $10,500. Factual: But for the accident, Margaret would not have had back pain and her doctor would not have prescribed opioids. Scope: The BCC will argue that Margaret broke the chain of causation by taking the opioids "beyond the prescribed dose", relying on Schneider, where the plaintiff's deliberate use of illegal drugs was not recoverable. However, this case can be distinguished from Schneider: Margaret's opioids were prescribed by her doctor as part of her treatment, and she began exceeding the dose only "as a result of the addiction", which itself developed from the prescribed use. It is also unlike Yates v Jones, because no third person suggested she take the drugs. Addiction is a reasonably foreseeable consequence of a strong prescribed painkiller, so on balance the addiction is within the scope of the BCC's liability and the $10,500 is likely to be recoverable.

This is the head that separates HD from Credit: argue both sides, compare with BOTH Schneider and Yates, then commit to an answer.

Defences

There are no facts suggesting Margaret was careless: she was walking to the bus stop and the car was out of control. Assumption: I assume she was on the footpath rather than walking on the road itself; if she was on the road, the BCC could argue contributory negligence and her damages could be reduced (Ingram v Britten). Voluntary assumption of risk is not relevant, as Margaret did not know about the pothole.

Conclusion

CONCLUSIONOn the balance of probabilities, Margaret can bring an action in negligence against the BCC and recover all three heads of loss, although the rehabilitation costs are the most open to dispute.

Remedies

Margaret will be awarded ordinary damages to restore her, so far as money can, to the position she would have been in had the BCC not been negligent. As damages are assessed "once and for all", she should also claim any future treatment costs and lost income now (the facts do not mention lost wages). She can claim $15,000 + $65,000 + $10,500 = $90,500.

Answer 3 — 2022 S2 Q1: Matt v Harry (speeding, drunk surgeon, PTSD, addiction — "all potential losses")

Question text — Semester Two 2022, Question 1 (25 marks)

Harry is running late for work. He gets in his car and drives as fast as he can from his home to the office. He takes the freeway where the speed limit is 100km/hr, but he drives at 175km/hr. As a result of his speeding, when changing lanes, he loses control of his vehicle and collides with another car on the road, causing serious injuries to the driver, Matt. At the time, Matt was distracted by his phone which he was holding in his right hand, while using his left hand to control the steering wheel.

Matt sustains serious injuries to both arms as well as his chest, resulting in $225,000 in medical expenses. During the operation, the surgeon, Dr Phillips, makes a number of critical mistakes which ultimately leaves Matt with a partial loss of sensation in his arms and a substantially reduced range of motion. As a result, Matt is unable to work for a year and loses $95,000 in wages. Following the operation, Matt lodges a complaint with the hospital, which investigates the matter and finds that the surgeon was intoxicated by alcohol at the time of the surgery.

After the car accident, Matt develops post-traumatic stress disorder, requiring psychiatric treatment, costing a further $35,000. His psychiatrist prescribes him strong medication for 2 months which he takes in accordance with the prescription. However, without the psychiatrist knowing, Matt continues to take the medication for a further 3 months and becomes addicted to it, which required him to spend an additional $7,500 on addiction therapy with a different psychiatrist.

Advise Harry as to any possible claims that Matt may have against him under Tort Law. In your answer, discuss all the potential losses that Matt may recover from Harry.

Answer plan (6 lines)
  1. Issue: Can Matt bring an action against Harry for the tort of negligence? (You are advising Harry — still write the issue from Matt's side.)
  2. Duty: motorists → other road users (established category); Donoghue neighbour principle.
  3. Breach: 175 in a 100 zone — s 9(1)(a)–(c), s 9(2) all point one way; Watt (no emergency, just late).
  4. Causation, 5 heads: medical $225k ✔ · surgeon's extra injury ✘ (Yates — sue Dr Phillips/hospital, Deatons v Flew) · wages $95k ✔ material contribution (Chappel, Cook) · PTSD $35k ✔ (Schneider) · addiction $7.5k ✘ (Schneider).
  5. Defences: contributory negligence — phone in hand (Manley, Ingram) → reduction.
  6. Remedies: ordinary damages ≈ $355,000 less contributory negligence; Harry not liable for $7,500 or the surgical injury.

Harry's liability to Matt depends on whether Matt can establish the tort of negligence against him and, because Matt has suffered several different losses, whether Harry's breach caused each one. The losses are: the medical expenses ($225,000); the further injury caused by Dr Phillips' surgery; lost wages ($95,000); PTSD treatment ($35,000); and addiction therapy ($7,500).

Issue

ISSUECan Matt bring an action against Harry for the tort of negligence?

Even though the question says "Advise Harry", the cause of action is Matt's. Phrase the issue as Matt v Harry, then advise Harry in the conclusion.

Law

A person commits the tort of negligence if they carelessly cause harm to another person. Matt must prove on the balance of probabilities that (1) Harry owed him a duty of care; (2) Harry breached it; and (3) the breach caused his loss. Defences and remedies are then considered. The Civil Liability Act 2003 (Qld) (CLA) applies.

Duty of care

Lord Atkin's neighbour principle in Donoghue v Stevenson [1932] AC 562 requires reasonable care to avoid acts or omissions that one can reasonably foresee would be likely to injure one's neighbour: persons so closely and directly affected that one ought reasonably to have them in contemplation. In that case, a manufacturer owed a duty to a consumer who became ill after drinking ginger beer containing a decomposed snail. A duty is readily found where the relationship falls into an established category; motorists owe a duty of care to other road users.

Breach of duty

Under s 9(1) CLA, a defendant breaches its duty if (a) the risk was foreseeable, (b) it was not insignificant, and (c) a reasonable person in the defendant's position would have taken precautions. Under s 9(2), the court considers the probability of harm, its likely seriousness, the burden of taking precautions and the social utility of the activity. In Bolton v Stone (1951) a cricket club was not liable because the chance of a ball leaving the ground and hitting someone was very small. By contrast, in Watt v Hertfordshire (1954) the social utility of an emergency response justified a fireman travelling with unsecured equipment.

Causation

Under s 11(1) CLA two tests must be satisfied for each loss. (a) Factual causation: "but for" the breach, would the harm have occurred? Where there are several causes, the material contribution test applies: the defendant is liable if its conduct increased the risk of injury and the risk eventuated, even if other factors also contributed (Chappel v Hart (1998); Cook v ACT Racing Club Inc [2001], where an overheated sauna was one of several causes of a heart attack). (b) Scope of liability: the type of harm must be reasonably foreseeable and not too remote (Overseas Tankships v Morts [1961]). An intervening act can break the chain of causation: in Yates v Jones [1990], an accident victim's heroin addiction was caused by the friend who suggested it, not the driver. In Schneider v Smith (2016), the plaintiff's deliberate, voluntary decision to take illegal drugs, which was not part of prescribed or recommended treatment, was not compensable; but medical and psychological treatment costs were, being ordinary and natural consequences of the accident.

Defences

Where the plaintiff contributed to his own injury, liability is apportioned between the parties (contributory negligence). In Ingram v Britten the plaintiff was driving a tractor at speed, although the defendant had failed to secure its frame; in Manley v Alexander the plaintiff drank 12 stubbies and slept in the road before the defendant negligently drove over him.

Vicarious liability

An employer may be liable for the acts of its employees committed in the course of their employment (Deatons v Flew (1949)).

Application

Duty of care

Harry and Matt were both driving on the freeway. As a motorist, Harry falls within the established category and owed Matt, another road user, a duty of care. It is plainly foreseeable under the neighbour principle that careless driving could injure other drivers.

Breach of duty

s 9(1)(a)–(b): It is foreseeable, and far from insignificant, that driving at 175 km/h, 75 km/h over the speed limit, could cause a driver to lose control and crash into another car. s 9(1)(c) and s 9(2): The probability of harm was high, especially when changing lanes on a busy freeway — unlike the remote risk in Bolton v Stone.
