To decide which a statement is, the court asks what a reasonable person would believe the parties intended about its contractual nature, having regard to: (1) the language used, since strong statements such as "I assure you" are more likely to be terms; (2) the context in which it was made; (3) the time it was made, since a statement immediately preceding the contract is more likely to be a term; (4) the maker of the statement and their relative knowledge and expertise; and (5) the importance of the statement, including whether the other party obviously relied on it. The lecture examples show that a precise, measurable claim, such as "this machine can process 500 units per hour", is more likely to be a term.

Parol evidence rule. Where a contract has been reduced to writing and appears to be entire, it is presumed that the writing contains all of its terms, and evidence of any previous or contemporaneous oral agreement will not be admitted to add to or vary it: Mercantile Bank of Sydney v Taylor. The rule only applies where the written contract appears to be entire. There are two main exceptions. First, where the contract was not intended to be wholly in writing, oral evidence may be used to establish oral terms. Second, the oral statement may form a collateral contract.

Collateral contract. A collateral contract is a separate, pre-existing contract formed by a promise that induces the other party to enter into the main contract. The elements are: (1) an oral statement and a main written contract; (2) the oral statement was intended as a promise, made to induce entry into the main contract; (3) the collateral contract is not inconsistent with the main written contract; and (4) entry into the main contract is the consideration for the promise: De Lassalle v Guildford. In that case, before the tenant handed over the signed lease, the landlord orally assured him that the drains were in good order. The lease said nothing about the drains, which proved defective. The assurance was enforced as a collateral contract. Because it is the collateral contract that is breached, the innocent party cannot terminate the main contract but may claim damages.

Case analysis in the Law section (facts + outcome of De Lassalle) is what moves an answer into the 6–7 band. Then compare it in Application.

Application

Term or representation. Language: Alex "assured" GlobalCorp that the software would handle "at least 10,000 concurrent users". This is a strong, specific and measurable statement, very like the lecture example "this machine can process 500 units per hour", and unlike vague opinions such as "this machine should be perfect for your business". Context: the statement was made in the course of lengthy commercial negotiations for a customised program, where scalability was a central concern. Timing: it was made one week before the contract was signed, i.e. close to formation, which suggests a term. Maker: Alex is the head of Technovate's technology department and based his statement on similar projects for other clients. He had expert knowledge that GlobalCorp lacked, so GlobalCorp was entitled to rely on him. Importance: the assurance "provided GlobalCorp with confidence in the software's scalability", and capacity was critical, since a 5,000-user limit was "not suitable" for GlobalCorp's operations. GlobalCorp obviously relied on it.

Technovate may argue that Alex was only giving an estimate based on past projects, and that because the parties signed a written contract that does not mention user capacity, they did not intend the statement to be contractual. However, the strength of the words, Alex's expertise and GlobalCorp's obvious reliance outweigh this. A reasonable person would regard the assurance as a promise.

Parol evidence rule. Technovate will argue that the written contract does not mention concurrent users, so the parol evidence rule prevents GlobalCorp from adding Alex's oral assurance to it. However, the rule only applies where the writing appears to be entire. Here the contract "lacks detail and is very poorly written" and "does not specifically address any of the points made by Technovate". There is no indication of an entire-agreement clause. Given six months of negotiations and the failure to record the key technical requirements, the better view is that the parties did not intend the contract to be wholly in writing, so the first exception applies and Alex's assurance may be proved as an oral term.

Collateral contract. In the alternative, GlobalCorp can rely on a collateral contract. (1) There is an oral statement by Alex and a main written contract. (2) The assurance was a promise ("would be capable of handling at least 10,000 concurrent users"), given to persuade GlobalCorp to sign, and it did induce GlobalCorp to contract. (3) It is not inconsistent with the written contract. It is consistent with, and adds detail to, the clause requiring "industry-standard performance capabilities". (4) GlobalCorp provided consideration by entering into the main contract. This is very similar to De Lassalle v Guildford: just as the landlord's assurance about the drains induced the tenant to sign a lease that was silent on drains, Alex's assurance about capacity induced GlobalCorp to sign a contract that was silent on capacity. The software handles only 5,000 users, so the promise has been broken.

Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, Alex's assurance is enforceable, either as a term of a contract not wholly in writing or, alternatively, as a collateral contract, and Technovate is in breach.

Issue 2: Damon's statements

Issue

Are Damon's statements that the software would "likely enhance productivity", was "the best on the market" and would "revolutionise GlobalCorp's business operations" enforceable terms of the contract?

Law

A "mere puff" is a statement that is clearly not intended to be taken seriously by the receiver, and it is often found in advertising, for example "Red Bull gives you wings to fly". It does not create a contractual obligation. In Carlill v Carbolic Smoke Ball Co, the company argued its advertisement was a mere puff, but the court held that it was a serious promise because the company had deposited £1,000 in the bank "showing our sincerity". Statements of opinion such as "I think you'll be very happy with it" are representations, not terms. The five factors set out in Issue 1 also apply.

Application

Damon's claims that the software was "the best on the market" and would "revolutionise" GlobalCorp's operations are exaggerated, unquantifiable sales talk of the kind a reasonable business would not take literally. Unlike Carlill, where the £1,000 deposit showed sincerity, Damon offered no research or statistics to back his claims. The statement that the software would "likely enhance productivity" is tentative ("likely") and an opinion about the future, similar to "I think you'll be very happy with it". Applying the factors, the language was weak, the statements were made at the beginning of the negotiation period, about six months before the contract was signed, and Damon was a junior sales representative rather than a technical expert. Nothing suggests GlobalCorp relied on these statements rather than on Alex's specific assurance. GlobalCorp might argue that productivity was important to it, but "enhanced productivity" is too vague to measure.

Conclusion

Damon's statements are mere puffery, or at most non-contractual representations, and are not enforceable terms of the contract.

The question says "Contract Law 2", so misrepresentation (Contract Law 3) and the ACL are not discussed. A single line noting the limit shows the marker you saw the boundary.

Issue 3: the written "industry-standard" clause

Issue

Has Technovate breached the express term requiring "industry-standard performance capabilities", and is that term a condition or a warranty?

Law

Express terms are those explicitly agreed by the parties, and a party is bound by the terms of a written contract it has signed: L'Estrange v Graucob. Once a term is identified, the court determines its importance. A condition is fundamental to the contract, and its breach entitles the innocent party to terminate and sue for damages: Poussard v Spiers, where a singer's absence from the opening performances allowed the producers to terminate. A warranty is a minor term whose breach gives damages only: Bettini v Gye, where missing rehearsals gave damages only. The test of essentiality asks whether the promise was so important to the innocent party that it would not have entered the contract without it: Associated Newspapers v Bancks, where a promise to publish a cartoonist's work on the front page was a condition.

Application

The clause is an express written term binding on Technovate. However, it is vague. Whether a 5,000-user capacity falls below "industry-standard performance capabilities" is a question of evidence that the facts do not supply. I assume expert evidence would be needed. GlobalCorp should therefore treat this clause as a supporting argument rather than its main claim. If Alex's assurance is a term (Issue 1), capacity of 10,000 users was essential to GlobalCorp. Like the front-page placement in Bancks and the opening performances in Poussard, it goes to the heart of the bargain, because software that cannot handle GlobalCorp's users is "not suitable" for its business. GlobalCorp would probably not have entered the contract without it, so it is likely a condition rather than a minor warranty as in Bettini v Gye.

Conclusion

The written clause is a term, but proving its breach is uncertain. The capacity promise, if it is a term, is likely a condition.

Remedies

Damages aim to place GlobalCorp in the position it would have been in had the contract been properly performed, and they are available for any breach of contract and for breach of a collateral contract. If Alex's assurance is a term and a condition, GlobalCorp may terminate the contract and claim damages, including its $250,000 of losses. If the assurance is enforced only as a collateral contract, GlobalCorp cannot terminate the main contract but may recover damages for the $250,000 loss caused by the software's inability to handle 10,000 users. GlobalCorp must take reasonable steps to mitigate its loss, for example by seeking an upgrade or a replacement system promptly: Payzu Ltd v Saunders. No remedy is available for Damon's statements. Where damages are inadequate, a court may order specific performance, requiring Technovate to deliver software meeting the promised capacity.

Overall: The most appropriate course of action is for GlobalCorp to sue Technovate for breach of Alex's assurance, as a term or, alternatively, as a collateral contract, and claim damages of $250,000. It should not rely on Damon's statements.

Model answer 2 · 2021 Semester 1 Q1: Melissa v BCC cloakroom disclaimer 15 marks (2021). Expand the application for 25

Question text

Melissa purchases a ticket to the musical "Cats", which is playing at Brisbane's Convention Centre (BCC).

On the evening of the performance, Melissa walks to the cloakroom (a secure place at the BCC in which people safely store their belongings) and asks for her expensive fur coat to be stored until the performance is over. The attendant of the cloakroom tells Melissa that it will cost $10 to store her coat in the cloakroom, and hands Melissa the cloakroom contract for her to sign. Before Melissa signs the contract, the attendant gives Melissa a separate piece of paper containing the terms and conditions of the cloakroom contract. Melissa replies "I don't need to read this", pays the $10 and signs the contract. Melissa is then told by the cloakroom attendant that her coat will be stored in Locker 5.

After the performance, Melissa returns to the cloakroom and notices that the cloakroom has been left unattended and the key to Locker 5 is lying out, visible, on the front desk. Melissa enters the cloakroom and sees that Locker 5 is open and her fur coat is missing.

The cloakroom attendant finally arrives back and apologies for his absence. He says that he was called away and must have left the key out on the desk. After a police investigation, it is determined that during his absence, a thief used the key to unlock the locker and steal Melissa's fur coat.

Melissa makes a claim against the BCC. However, the BCC argues that they are not liable because of a disclaimer that is clearly visible in the document containing the terms and conditions of the cloakroom contract. The disclaimer reads:

The Brisbane Convention Centre accepts no responsibility for any loss or damage to the property of any of its patrons whilst on their premises, however that loss or damage may arise.

Advise Melissa as to whether BCC will be liable for the loss of her coat, or whether the BCC can rely upon the disclaimer to avoid liability. (Note: for the purpose of this exercise you may assume that a contract has been formed.)

Answer plan
  1. Opener: two issues (term? applies to breach?). The contract is assumed, so no formation discussion.
  2. Issue 1: signed contract (L'Estrange; the T&Cs were a separate unsigned page, so the argument goes both ways) → reasonable notice before formation (Olley / Thornton distinguished: handed over before she signed and paid) → prior dealings not needed. Conclusion: it IS a term.
  3. Issue 2: B2C → contra proferentem (White v John Warwick): "however … may arise" doesn't clearly cover BCC's own negligence.
  4. Scope (Sydney City Council v West): the key left on the desk is outside the purpose of secure storage. Near-identical facts.
  5. Conclusion plus remedies: damages for the coat's value; mitigation.

Whether the disclaimer will effectively protect the BCC from liability for breach of contract depends upon two separate issues: (1) whether the disclaimer is in fact a term of the cloakroom contract; and (2) whether the disclaimer will be interpreted as applying to the breach in question. As instructed, it is assumed that a contract was formed between Melissa and the BCC when she paid $10 to store her coat.

Issue 1

Issue

Is the disclaimer a term of the cloakroom contract between Melissa and the BCC?

Law

A disclaimer is a term of a contract stating that one of the parties will not be in breach despite failing to perform one or more of its contractual obligations. Disclaimers are also known as exclusion, exemption or limitation of liability clauses. A disclaimer will be a term of the contract if it is: (a) incorporated into a written contract that has been signed; (b) brought to the other party's attention by reasonable notice before the contract is formed; or (c) implied into the contract as a result of prior dealings.

Signed contract. A party who signs a written contract is bound by its terms whether or not they have read them: L'Estrange v Graucob. In that case, Miss L'Estrange signed an order form for a vending machine without reading the small print, which excluded all warranties. When the machine failed, she was bound by the exclusion clause.

Reasonable notice. With an unsigned document, an exclusion clause is binding only if it was brought to the other party's attention by reasonable notice before the contract was entered into: Olley v Marlborough Court. There, a hotel's disclaimer was on a notice in the guests' bedroom, which they saw only after the contract had been made at the reception desk, so it was not a term. Similarly, in Thornton v Shoe Lane Parking, conditions referred to on a car-park ticket were not a term because the contract had already been formed at the ticket machine. The court asks whether the document is of a kind a reasonable person would expect to contain contractual terms, and whether the business did what was reasonable to bring the terms to the customer's attention.

Application

Signed contract. Melissa signed the cloakroom contract. The disclaimer appears in a separate piece of paper containing the terms and conditions of that contract, which Melissa did not sign. The BCC will argue that, because the terms and conditions document formed part of the cloakroom contract and was handed over as part of the signing process, Melissa is bound by it as part of the signed contract. Similar to L'Estrange v Graucob, Melissa's decision not to read it ("I don't need to read this") does not help her. Melissa may argue that the disclaimer was not in the document she actually signed. The facts do not say whether the signed contract referred to the separate terms. I assume it did, since the document is described as containing "the terms and conditions of the cloakroom contract".

Reasonable notice. Even if the separate page is treated as an unsigned document, the disclaimer was brought to Melissa's attention by reasonable notice before the contract was formed. The attendant handed her the terms before she signed and before she paid the $10, and the disclaimer was "clearly visible" in that document. A document headed as the terms and conditions of a contract is exactly the kind of document a reasonable person would expect to contain contractual terms. This is unlike Olley and Thornton, where the terms were only seen after the contract had been formed. Here, the BCC did everything reasonable to give notice, and Melissa chose not to read it.

Prior dealings. There is no evidence that Melissa had used the cloakroom before, so prior dealings (Balmain New Ferry v Robertson) do not need to be relied upon.

Conclusion

On the balance of probabilities, the disclaimer is a term of the cloakroom contract, because it was part of the contract Melissa signed or, alternatively, was brought to her attention by reasonable notice before the contract was formed.

Issue 2

Issue

Will the disclaimer protect the BCC from liability for the theft of Melissa's fur coat?

Law

Where a disclaimer is contained in a contract between two businesses, the courts presume equal bargaining power and interpret it neutrally. However, where the disclaimer is uncertain or ambiguous and is contained in a contract between a business and a consumer, the court will interpret it contra proferentem, that is, against the interests of the party seeking to rely on it. If there is more than one way of interpreting the disclaimer, the court will choose the interpretation that makes it less likely that the disclaimer applies to the breach: White v John Warwick. In that case, a clause in a tricycle hire agreement that "nothing in this agreement shall render the owners liable for any personal injuries" was read narrowly, as excluding only contractual liability and not liability for the owners' negligence when the seat came loose and injured the hirer.

Further, a disclaimer will not exclude liability for acts occurring outside the scope of the contract: Sydney City Council v West. There, a car-park attendant negligently allowed a person to take a car without producing proof of a ticket. Despite a widely drafted clause excluding responsibility for loss "however … caused", the council was liable, because the attendant's act was outside the scope of the contract.

Application

Contra proferentem. The cloakroom contract is between a business, the BCC, and a consumer, Melissa, so the disclaimer will be read against the BCC. The disclaimer is ambiguous. It is unclear whether "any loss or damage to the property of any of its patrons … however that loss or damage may arise" was intended to cover loss caused by patrons themselves or by unavoidable and unforeseen events, or whether it extends to loss caused by the BCC's own careless or negligent conduct. The clause does not expressly mention negligence. As there is more than one interpretation, the court will choose the one that goes against the BCC. Similar to White v John Warwick, where general words did not exclude the owners' negligence, the BCC will remain liable for loss caused by its own fault: its attendant leaving the cloakroom unattended with the key to Locker 5 lying in view on the front desk.

Scope. Melissa will argue that the whole purpose of the cloakroom contract was the secure storage of her belongings. The cloakroom is described as "a secure place … in which people safely store their belongings", and she paid $10 for that service. This is almost identical to Sydney City Council v West. Just as the purpose of the car park was that only the customer could retrieve the car on producing a ticket, the purpose of the cloakroom was that only Melissa could retrieve her coat from a locked locker. Leaving the cloakroom unattended with the locker key on display, so that a thief could simply pick it up and open Locker 5, is a negligent act that is the opposite of what the contract required. It falls outside the scope of what a reasonable person would contemplate occurring within this contract. The BCC may argue that "however that loss or damage may arise" is broad enough to cover theft, but the clause in West was equally broad and still did not protect the council.

Conclusion

The stronger argument is that, although the disclaimer is a term of the contract, it does not protect the BCC from liability for its own negligent conduct, which was outside the scope of the contract. Therefore the BCC is liable for the loss of Melissa's coat.

Remedies

Melissa may claim damages for breach of contract, which aim to put her in the position she would have been in had the contract been performed, i.e. had her coat been returned safely. She can therefore recover the value of the fur coat. The facts do not state its value, so evidence of its market value or replacement cost will be needed. Melissa must take reasonable steps to mitigate her loss (Payzu Ltd v Saunders), for example by cooperating with the police in case the coat is recovered.

For a 25-mark version, make Issue 1 two-sided in more depth (signed vs unsigned page) and add a line distinguishing Balmain. The structure is the W7 Paula / GCGR exemplar almost line for line, which is exactly what the marker wants.

Model answer 3 · Tutorial 5 homework: Jack & Mary painting (Contract Law 1 only)

Question text

Jack is an art collector. He likes to buy expensive paintings. One day his sister, Mary, invites him over to her house for coffee. During coffee, Jack notices an expensive painting on the wall of her home. Mary tells Jack she inherited it from her best friend.

Jack asks Mary, "Would you be willing to sell it to me?" Mary replies, "I actually have it listed online for sale on Ebay for $150,000. However, you can buy it for $140,000." Jack says, "$140,000 sounds a little expensive. How about $120,000?" Mary responds, "Yes. That's a deal. I will deliver it to your address by next week. Is that okay?" Jack nods and writes Mary a cheque for $120,000, which he gives to her.

On the way home, Jack remembers that the painting was surrounded by a very old and damaged frame. When he gets home, he phones Mary to ask if she could have a new frame put on the painting. Mary says, "Yes, I will have a new frame put on the painting for you. The new frame will be delivered with the painting next week."

When the painting arrives the following week, Jack sees that it is still surrounded by the old frame. Jack is upset. He phones Mary and says that he either wants his money back, or wants the painting re-framed.

Advise Mary if she formed a contract with Jack for the sale of the painting. Also advise Mary whether her promise to re-frame the painting can be enforced. In your answer, only discuss the content in Contract Law 1. You must use ILAC to answer this problem.

Answer plan (the marker's feedback: identify BOTH counter-offers; analyse Wakeling v Ripley for intention and Roscorla v Thomas for past consideration; treat the two promises SEPARATELY)
  1. Issue 1: was a contract for the sale of the painting formed? Law on the 3 elements, with Carlill, Felthouse, Wakeling, Ermogenous, Thomas.
  2. Agreement: eBay listing = invitation to treat; the $140k offer → Jack's $120k counter-offer → Mary's "Yes, deal, I'll deliver next week, OK?" (a further counter-offer adding delivery) → Jack's nod plus cheque = acceptance by conduct.
  3. Intention: siblings, but 5+ business-like facts; compare Wakeling.
  4. Consideration: $120k cheque v painting plus delivery → contract formed.
  5. Issue 2: re-frame promise → the cheque is past consideration (Roscorla), no fresh consideration → unenforceable. Advice to Mary: keep the money; no obligation to re-frame.

Mary's position depends upon two separate issues: (1) whether a contract was formed between Jack and Mary for the sale of the painting; and (2) whether Mary's later promise to re-frame the painting is enforceable by Jack.

Issue 1

Issue

Was a contract formed between Jack and Mary for the sale of the painting?

Law

A contract is a legally binding promise or agreement. A simple contract may be made verbally, in writing or by conduct, and it requires three elements: (1) agreement; (2) intention to create legal relations; and (3) consideration.

Element 1: Agreement. Agreement consists of an offer plus an acceptance. An offer is a proposal by one party to another to immediately enter into a legally binding contract, assessed objectively by asking whether a reasonable person in the recipient's position would regard it as an offer. An offer must be distinguished from an invitation to treat, such as most advertisements and price lists, which merely invites others to make offers. However, an advertisement may be an offer to the world where it shows a willingness to be bound immediately: Carlill v Carbolic Smoke Ball Co. Acceptance occurs when the offeree indicates, by words or conduct, a final and unqualified assent to all the terms of the offer. Acceptance must be unqualified, otherwise it will be considered a counter-offer, which rejects the original offer and puts forward a new offer on different terms. A counter-offer differs from a mere rejection, which simply refuses the offer. Acceptance is judged objectively and may be made by conduct, but silence alone is not acceptance: Felthouse v Bindley.

Element 2: Intention. The parties must intend to create legal relations, and the onus is on the party alleging the contract to prove it. Traditionally, courts presumed that social or domestic agreements were not intended to be binding, but this presumption could be rebutted: Wakeling v Ripley. In that case, Ripley, an elderly man living in Sydney, persuaded his sister and her husband to leave England and move to Sydney to look after him, promising they could live in his house and would inherit it. When he later evicted them, the court held that, despite the family relationship, there was an intention to be legally bound, given the seriousness of the promises and the significant sacrifices made in reliance on them. Today this is an objective test, and the correct approach is to consider the agreement on its own merits without presumptions. All of the relevant circumstances and the parties' conduct are considered, including the conduct of the parties, the context in which the agreement was made, the language used, the value of any consideration and the nature of the relationship: Ermogenous v Greek Orthodox Community of SA Inc.

Element 3: Consideration. The common law requires that, for an agreement to be binding, the promisee must provide consideration for the promise received. Consideration is the price paid, or promised, by each party for the other's promise, and it may take the form of money, goods or services, or a promise to provide them: Carlill v Carbolic Smoke Ball Co. It need not be adequate, only of some legal value: Thomas v Thomas.

Application

To determine whether a contract was formed between Jack and Mary, it is necessary to apply the three elements.

Element 1: Agreement. Jack's question, "Would you be willing to sell it to me?", was a mere inquiry, not an offer. Mary's eBay listing at $150,000 was an advertisement and, unlike the advertisement in Carlill, merely an invitation to treat. The offer was made when Mary said, "you can buy it for $140,000": a firm, certain proposal, communicated to Jack, to sell immediately at a stated price. A counter-offer was made when Jack replied, "How about $120,000?" This was not a rejection but a counter-offer, because it rejected the $140,000 offer while proposing a new price, and Mary's original offer was then no longer open for acceptance. A further counter-offer occurred when Mary responded, "Yes. That's a deal. I will deliver it to your address by next week. Is that okay?" Although Mary said "yes", she added a new term about delivery and asked for Jack's agreement, so her response was qualified. Acceptance occurred when Jack nodded and handed Mary a cheque for $120,000. This was acceptance by conduct, objectively showing his final and unqualified assent to the sale at $120,000 with delivery by next week. It was not silence, as in Felthouse v Bindley, but positive acts. Alternatively, if Mary's "Yes. That's a deal" is treated as an acceptance of Jack's $120,000 offer, agreement was reached at that point. Either way, there was agreement.

Element 2: Intention. The correct approach is to consider the agreement between Jack and Mary on its own merits, viewing all the conduct objectively. Mary may argue that, as siblings chatting over coffee at her home, they were in a social or domestic setting and did not intend legal consequences. However, the nature of the relationship is no longer decisive (Ermogenous). As was the case in Wakeling v Ripley, where the court found that family members intended to be bound because the arrangement was serious and significant, the parties here clearly intended to be legally bound. Various facts support this. (1) The value of the consideration was very large, $120,000. (2) The parties engaged in genuine price negotiation from $140,000 down to $120,000, typical of a commercial bargain. (3) Mary used commercial language: "That's a deal." (4) Jack immediately wrote a cheque for the full price, a formal step. (5) The parties arranged delivery to Jack's address by a set time. (6) The painting was already listed for sale on the open market on eBay, showing Mary was dealing with it commercially. (7) Jack is an art collector who buys expensive paintings, so this was the type of transaction he enters into in a business-like way. Just as the Wakelings' sacrifices showed seriousness in Wakeling, Jack's payment of $120,000 shows the seriousness of this arrangement.

Element 3: Consideration. Jack provided consideration in the form of the $120,000 cheque. Mary provided consideration in the form of her promise to transfer the painting and deliver it to Jack's address. This is sufficient consideration. Even though the price was below the eBay listing, consideration need not be adequate (Thomas v Thomas).

Conclusion

A court would likely decide that a contract was formed between Jack and Mary for the sale of the painting for $120,000, including delivery by the following week.

Issue 2

Issue

Is Mary's promise to re-frame the painting enforceable by Jack?

Law

A promise is only enforceable as part of a contract if the promisee has provided consideration for it. Consideration must be sufficient, and past consideration is not good consideration. An act or payment completed before a promise is made cannot be the price of that promise: Roscorla v Thomas. In that case, after the sale of a horse had been concluded, the seller promised the buyer that the horse was sound and free from vice. The horse proved vicious, but the buyer could not enforce the promise, because the only consideration he had given, the purchase price, had been provided before the promise was made.

Application

Mary's promise to re-frame the painting is separate from her promise to sell and deliver it. The contract of sale was complete when Jack accepted and gave Mary the cheque at her home. Mary's promise to re-frame was made later, when Jack phoned her after arriving home. The payment by Jack for the purchase of the painting was made before Mary's promise. Therefore that payment is past consideration and cannot support the new promise. This is very similar to Roscorla v Thomas. Just as the seller's promise about the horse's soundness was made only after the sale had been concluded and the price paid, Mary's promise to put a new frame on the painting was made only after the sale had been agreed and the $120,000 handed over. Jack did not offer any additional payment or anything else in return for the new frame. Ultimately, no consideration was provided by Jack for Mary's promise to re-frame the painting.

Conclusion

Mary's promise to re-frame the painting cannot be enforced, as past consideration is not sufficient consideration.

Advice to Mary

Mary formed a binding contract to sell the painting to Jack for $120,000 and has performed it by delivering the painting by the following week. Jack is not entitled to his money back, as there has been no breach of the contract of sale, and Mary is not legally obliged to re-frame the painting.

The question limited the answer to Contract Law 1, so promissory estoppel (Contract Law 2) is deliberately NOT discussed. Even if it were allowed, Jack suffered no real detriment in reliance. The markers specifically rewarded naming BOTH counter-offers and calling the cheque "past consideration" explicitly.

T5 IP · from t5-ip.html

4 · Full model answers

Model 1 — 2021 S1 Q2: Alex and Travis (copyright)

Question text (15 marks in 2021; write it as a full 25-mark answer)

Alex is a law student at the University of Brisbane (UB). One day Alex wakes up late and realises he cannot make it to his 8am lecture. Alex phones his classmate, Travis, and asks Travis to secretly record the lecture with a digital voice recorder so that Alex can listen to it later. UB has a policy that they do not record any lectures.

The lecture goes for two hours and is divided into two sessions. During the first hour, Alex's usual lecturer, Professor Butler, presents a lecture on the topic of Contract Law. The lecture is given by reading out a set of comprehensive teaching notes which Professor Butler prepared prior to class.

During the second hour, Professor Butler invites another academic at UB named Charley to talk to the students. Charley is also a corporate lawyer who gives an unscripted presentation on real-world Contract Law issues whilst answering various questions from students about his legal experiences.

The day after the lecture, Travis sends Alex the recording. Alex listens to it, and then also sells the recording to other students who also could not attend the lecture.

Advise Alex and Travis whether they have committed an intellectual property breach. Explore all aspects of the law including copyright protection, ownership and whether there are any relevant defences.

Answer plan (5 lines)
  1. Issue 1 Protection — split the lecture: Butler's hour (written notes = literary work, original, material form ✅) vs Charley's hour (unscripted → material form is the battleground; s 10 + Donoghue; two sides).
  2. Issue 2 Ownership — Butler = UB employee → s 35(6) UB owns (but uni IP policy may let academics keep it). Charley = UB academic, but is a guest talk within his duties? Argue both; state the assumption.
  3. Issue 3 Infringement — s 31 rights: Travis reproduced it (recording); Alex procured it, then published/communicated it by selling. Substantial part (whole lecture — Hawkes, EMI); objective similarity (exact copy — vs Zeccola); causal connection (direct, deliberate — Bright Tunes).
  4. Issue 4 Defences — fair dealing for research/study: arguable for Alex's private listening; fails for Travis's secret recording against policy (arguably) and clearly fails for the sales.
  5. Remedies s 115 — injunction; damages or account of profits (Alex's sales). One-line overall conclusion.

Whether Alex and Travis have committed an intellectual property breach depends on whether copyright protects the lecture, who owns that copyright, whether Alex and Travis infringed it, and whether any defence applies.

Issue 1: Protection

Issue
Is the lecture given by Professor Butler and Charley protected by copyright under the Copyright Act 1968 (Cth)?

Law
Intellectual property is an intangible product of intellectual effort, and IP law gives its owner the right to prevent others from copying, using or exploiting it without permission. Copyright is the right to prevent unauthorised copying of the expression of an idea; it does not protect the idea itself. Copyright is regulated by the Copyright Act 1968 (Cth) ("CA") and arises automatically, without registration. A creation is protected if: (1) it is a 'work' or 'subject matter other than works'; (2) it is original; and (3) it is expressed in a material form.

Work. Section 32 protects original literary, dramatic, musical and artistic works. A literary work is the expression of an idea in text, such as a book, article, manual or written information (Mirror Newspapers v Queensland Newspapers [1982]). The CA also separately protects 'subject matter other than works', including sound recordings (s 89), so several layers of copyright may exist in the one product.

Original. A work is original if it originates from the author through independent intellectual effort rather than being copied. It need not be new or unique, and the threshold is low (s 32).

Material form. Copyright protects the expression of an idea, not ideas or facts themselves (John Fairfax & Sons v Australian Consolidated Press (1960)). 'Material form' means 'any form (whether visible or not) of storage' (s 10). In Donoghue v Allied Newspapers (1938), a jockey told his stories to a journalist, who wrote them up. The court held that the journalist, who created the particular expression, was the author; the jockey had no copyright merely because he supplied the ideas orally.

Case analysis in the Law section — facts + held — so you can compare in Application. This is what pushes a 5 to a 6/7 (Tutorial 4 feedback).

Application
Professor Butler's session. The lecture was delivered by reading out comprehensive teaching notes that Professor Butler prepared before class. Those notes are written text expressing his ideas on Contract Law, so they are a literary work under s 32. They are original, because Professor Butler prepared them himself using his own skill and effort, which easily meets the low threshold. They were also in material form, being written and stored before the lecture (s 10). Copyright therefore subsists in the notes, and reading them aloud is a reproduction of that literary work.

Charley's session. Charley's presentation was unscripted, and he answered students' questions spontaneously. His spoken words are likely original, because they come from his own legal experience and are not copied. Material form, however, is arguable. On one view, the talk was never stored in any form by Charley, so, like an idea that is merely communicated, it is not protected. On the other view, Travis's recording stored the words in a material form (s 10), so a literary work then existed. Applying Donoghue, authorship depends on who created the particular expression. Here that was Charley, who chose the words, not Travis, who merely pressed record and contributed no expression. This is the reverse of Donoghue, where the person who only supplied ideas orally was not the author. On balance, it is likely that copyright subsists in Charley's words as a literary work once recorded, with Charley as their author.

The recording itself. Travis's recording is a sound recording, which is 'subject matter other than works' (s 89). That does not give Travis any right to use the lecture content recorded in it, which is a separate layer of copyright.

Conclusion
Copyright likely subsists in Professor Butler's notes as a literary work, and probably also in Charley's presentation once it was recorded, although this is less certain.

Issue 2: Ownership

Issue
Who owns the copyright in Professor Butler's and Charley's material?

Law
Ownership of copyright is separate from ownership of a physical object. The author is generally the first owner (s 35(2)). However, where a work is created by an employee in pursuance of the terms of their employment, the employer generally owns the copyright (s 35(6)). The creator must be an employee rather than an independent contractor, and the work must be made under their employment duties (Redrock Holdings v Hinkley (2001)). These default rules can be changed by agreement (s 35(3)), and university IP policies may allow academics to keep copyright in certain scholarly works.

Application
Butler. Professor Butler is UB's "usual lecturer", which indicates he is a UB employee. Preparing and delivering teaching notes is a central part of a lecturer's duties, so the notes were created in pursuance of his employment, and under s 35(6) UB likely owns the copyright. However, the facts do not say whether UB has an IP policy or agreement letting academics keep copyright in teaching materials (s 35(3)). If it does, Professor Butler would own it. Either way, the owner is not Alex or Travis.

Charley. Charley is "another academic at UB", so he is also an employee. Speaking to students at a colleague's invitation is arguably within an academic's teaching duties, in which case UB owns the copyright under s 35(6). Against this, the talk drew on his separate work as a corporate lawyer and was a one-off guest appearance, so it may not have been "in pursuance of" his UB employment, in which case Charley owns it under s 35(2). Since the facts are silent, I assume it was part of his UB duties.

Conclusion
UB is likely the owner of the copyright in both sessions, unless an IP policy or agreement provides otherwise, or Charley's talk fell outside his duties, in which case he owns his part.

Issue 3: Infringement

Issue
Have Travis and Alex infringed the copyright in the lecture?

Law
Under s 31 of the CA, the owner has the exclusive rights to reproduce the work in a material form, publish it, perform it in public, communicate it to the public and make an adaptation. Infringement occurs when a person exercises these rights without permission, and requires: (1) a substantial part was copied; (2) objective similarity; and (3) a causal connection.

Substantial part is a qualitative test of the importance of what was copied, not its amount. In Hawkes & Son v Paramount Film Service (1934), a short but distinctive part of a musical work in a film was substantial. In EMI Songs Australia v Larrikin Music (2011), only two bars of "Kookaburra" in "Down Under" were still substantial.

Objective similarity compares the particular expression, not the general idea. In Zeccola v Universal Studios (1982), "Great White" copied the characters, situations and sequence of events of "Jaws" and so infringed.

Causal connection requires that the copy be derived from the original rather than created independently. It may be inferred from access, and need not be deliberate: in Bright Tunes v Harrisongs Music (1976), George Harrison subconsciously copied "He's So Fine", which he had heard before.

Application
Exclusive rights. By recording the lecture on a digital voice recorder, Travis reproduced Professor Butler's and Charley's works in a material form, without the permission of UB, which has a policy against recording. Alex asked Travis to make the recording, and then sold copies to other students. By doing so he published the work, or communicated it to the public, and so exercised the owner's exclusive rights himself.

Substantial part. The whole two-hour lecture was recorded. If two bars were substantial in EMI v Larrikin and a short extract in Hawkes, then the entire lecture, including all of its key content, clearly is.

Objective similarity. A sound recording is an exact reproduction of the words spoken. Unlike Zeccola, where the court had to compare plot and characters to find similarity, here the copy is identical.

Causal connection. Travis recorded the lecture directly as it was given, and Alex's copies were made from Travis's recording, so the copies were plainly derived from the original. The copying was deliberate and secret, which is a stronger case than Bright Tunes, where even subconscious copying sufficed.

Conclusion
Travis has likely infringed copyright by reproducing the lecture, and Alex by procuring the recording and then selling it to other students, unless a defence applies.

Issue 4: Defences

Issue
Can Travis or Alex rely on the defence of fair dealing?

Law
A defendant may argue that no substantial part was copied, or rely on an exception under the CA. The fair dealing provisions (ss 40–43, 103A–104) permit use of copyright material without permission where the dealing is fair and for a recognised purpose, including research or study, criticism or review, parody or satire, reporting news, or judicial proceedings or professional advice. Whether a dealing is fair depends on the circumstances, including the purpose and extent of the use.

Application
The "no substantial part" defence fails, because the whole lecture was copied. As for fair dealing, Alex may argue that the recording was made so he could study the lecture he missed, which is a purpose of research or study. In favour of fairness, he is an enrolled student and missed a lecture he was entitled to attend. Against this, the entire lecture was copied, it was done "secretly" in breach of UB's policy against recording, and Travis made it for someone else rather than for his own study. These factors suggest the dealing was not fair. In any event, Alex's sale of the recording to other students is a commercial purpose, not research or study, and so falls outside fair dealing altogether.

Conclusion
Fair dealing might arguably cover Alex's private listening, but it will not protect Travis's secret recording, and it clearly cannot excuse Alex's sales.

Remedies

UB (or Charley, for his part, if he is the owner) may seek an injunction to stop further copying and sales (s 115(2)). It may also seek either damages for any loss caused (s 115(2)–(3)) or an account of the profits Alex made from selling the recording (s 115(2)–(3)). Travis and Alex should stop distributing the recording and delete all copies.

Overall: On the balance of probabilities, both Travis and Alex have infringed copyright in the lecture, which is most likely owned by UB, and Alex's sales attract the greatest liability.

≈1,450 words. Moves that earn marks: splitting the two sessions, arguing both sides of material form and of Charley's ownership, comparing a case in every element, and stating assumptions where facts are silent. The question says "explore all aspects including … ownership … defences", so all four issues + remedies are required.

Model 2 — Practice question (25 marks): trade mark + passing off

MoonBean Pty Ltd runs a popular chain of three coffee shops in Brisbane. In 2019 it registered the word mark "MOONBEAN" and a logo of a gold crescent moon on a navy circle as trade marks for coffee and café services. It also serves every drink in a distinctive navy cup with a gold band, which is not registered.

In 2026 Ruby, who worked as a barista at a MoonBean shop for two years until 2025, opens a café 500 m from one of MoonBean's shops called "MoonBeam Coffee". Her sign shows a gold crescent moon on a navy background, and she serves coffee in navy cups with a gold band. When asked, Ruby says she "just likes the moon". In its first month, several customers post online: "Didn't know MoonBean opened a new store!" and one says "pretty sure it's not the same owners, but the cups look identical."

Advise MoonBean whether it has any action against Ruby under trade mark law and the tort of passing off. (Do not consider the ACL.)

Answer plan
  1. Issue 1 TM infringement: registered ✅ → deceptively similar (one letter; same moon device; Torpedoes v Thorpedo; evidence of confusion) → same services ✅ → defences (not her own name, not descriptive, no prior use).
  2. Remedies under the TMA.
  3. Issue 2 Passing off (the cup is unregistered, so it needs passing off): misrepresentation of connection (Pacific Dunlop v Hogan; counter: McWilliam's — confusion ≠ enough) → in trade → intention to deceive (former employee, several features copied).
  4. Remedies: damages + injunction.
  5. Conclusion per issue.

MoonBean's claims against Ruby depend on two issues: first, whether Ruby has infringed MoonBean's registered trade marks; and second, whether Ruby has committed the tort of passing off, which is particularly relevant to MoonBean's unregistered cup design.

Issue 1: Trade mark infringement

Issue
Can MoonBean bring an action against Ruby for infringement of its registered trade marks under the Trade Marks Act 1995 (Cth)?

Law
Intellectual property law gives the owner of IP the right to prevent others from copying or exploiting it without permission. A trade mark is a distinctive word, phrase or symbol used in commercial dealings to show a connection between a business and its product (s 17 TMA). It may consist of letters, a word, a name, a device, packaging, a shape or a colour. For example, in Coca-Cola v All-Fect (1999), Coca-Cola's registered contour-bottle shape allowed it to stop All-Fect selling lollies in a bottle of the same shape. Trade marks must be registered to be protected under the TMA, and protection extends to the classes of goods and services for which the mark is registered.

A registered trade mark is infringed if: (1) the owner's trade mark is registered under the TMA; (2) the defendant uses a mark that is substantially identical with or deceptively similar to it; and (3) the defendant uses it in relation to goods or services the same as or closely related to those for which it is registered. A mark is deceptively similar if it nearly resembles the registered mark and is likely to mislead or cause confusion (s 44). In Torpedoes v Thorpedo (2003), the mark "THORPEDO" was held deceptively similar to the registered mark "TORPEDOES" for clothing, despite the different spelling and its link to Ian Thorpe's name.

A defendant may avoid liability if the mark is their own name or place of business; describes the kind, quality or another characteristic of their products; is used in comparative advertising; is used with the owner's implied consent; or has been used since before the owner registered or used it.

Application
Registered. MoonBean registered both the word "MOONBEAN" and its gold-crescent-on-navy logo in 2019 for coffee and café services, so the first element is satisfied.

Deceptively similar. "MoonBeam" differs from "MOONBEAN" by a single letter, and when spoken the two are almost indistinguishable. Similar to Torpedoes v Thorpedo, where a minor spelling change did not prevent deceptive similarity, the small change here does not alter the overall impression. Ruby's logo also uses the same device (a gold crescent moon) on the same navy background as MoonBean's registered logo. There is also evidence of actual confusion: several customers believed that "MoonBean opened a new store", which shows the mark is likely to mislead or cause confusion. Ruby may argue that "beam" and "bean" are different words with different meanings, and that a crescent moon is a common image. However, the combination of a near-identical name and the same logo, colours and layout makes this argument weak.

Same services. Ruby uses the mark for a coffee shop, which is exactly the coffee and café services for which MoonBean's marks are registered. Because the services are the same, MoonBean does not need to show its mark is well known in Australia.

Defences. "MoonBeam" is not Ruby's own name or place of business, and it does not describe the kind or quality of her coffee. MoonBean has not consented. Ruby has only used the mark since 2026, well after MoonBean's 2019 registration, so the prior-use defence does not apply. Her explanation that she "just likes the moon" is not a recognised defence.

Conclusion
Ruby has likely infringed MoonBean's registered word mark and logo.

Remedies
Under the TMA, MoonBean may obtain an injunction stopping Ruby from using the name and logo, and either damages for its lost business or an account of the profits Ruby made using the mark.

Defences are worth marks in IP. Run through the list quickly and knock out each one with a fact.

Issue 2: Passing off

Issue
Can MoonBean bring an action against Ruby for the tort of passing off?

Law
Where a trade mark or get-up is unregistered, the owner may still prevent its misuse through the tort of passing off. A defendant commits passing off if they misrepresent themselves or their product as having some kind of connection with the plaintiff, for example by copying another's trade mark. The plaintiff must show that: (1) the defendant made a misrepresentation, express or implied, that its goods or services are connected with the plaintiff or have the plaintiff's endorsement or approval; (2) the misrepresentation was made in the course of trade; and (3) the misrepresentation was intended to deceive potential purchasers.

In Pacific Dunlop v Hogan (1989), a shoe advertisement parodied the famous knife scene from Crocodile Dundee. Paul Hogan had not agreed to it, but viewers would assume he had endorsed or was connected with the shoes, so it was held to be passing off. A connection need not be stated expressly; it may be implied from the overall impression created. However, mere confusion, without a belief in a connection, may be insufficient, as shown by the overlapping s 18 case of McWilliam's Wines v McDonald's (1980).

Application
Misrepresentation. Ruby never expressly claimed to be part of MoonBean. However, by using a near-identical name, the same moon logo, and the same distinctive navy cups with a gold band, she impliedly represented that her café is connected with MoonBean, for example as a new branch. As in Pacific Dunlop v Hogan, where viewers inferred an endorsement that was never stated, customers here actually inferred a connection, posting that MoonBean had "opened a new store". Ruby may argue, relying on McWilliam's, that some customers were merely confused. One customer was "pretty sure it's not the same owners". But the test does not require every customer to be deceived, and several customers positively believed there was a connection. This is more than confusion.

In the course of trade. The name, logo and cups are used in selling coffee to the public, so this element is clearly satisfied.

Intention to deceive. Ruby worked at MoonBean for two years, so she knew its name, logo and cup design well. She then opened 500 m from a MoonBean shop and adopted not one but three of its features: name, logo and cups. It is difficult to accept that this was coincidence, and an intention to deceive potential purchasers can be inferred. Against this, Ruby says she "just likes the moon". However, that explains neither the identical cup design nor her choice of a name one letter away from her former employer's. Although the facts do not reveal her private motive, the circumstances strongly indicate an intention to attract MoonBean's customers.

Conclusion
On the balance of probabilities, Ruby has committed the tort of passing off. This is particularly important for the unregistered cup design, which trade mark law does not protect.

Remedies
MoonBean may seek damages to compensate for lost sales and place it in the position it would have been in had the tort not been committed, and an injunction restraining Ruby from continuing to use the name, logo and cup get-up.

Overall: MoonBean is likely to succeed against Ruby both for registered trade mark infringement (name and logo) and in passing off (name, logo and cups).

≈1,250 words. If the exam allowed the ACL, you'd add a third issue under s 18 (Taco Bell tests) — see T6.

T2 Other torts · from t2-other-torts.html

5 · Full model answers (3 fresh 25-mark practice questions)

These are practice questions written for this pack (no past paper has been purely on these torts). Every authority used is from the W3/W4/W8/W9 slides. Each answer is about 1,300–1,700 words, which is realistic for 55 minutes by hand.

Practice Question A · Bargain Barn and the false alarm (trespass to the person)

Bargain Barn Pty Ltd operates a discount department store in Chermside, Brisbane. It employs Trent as an in-store security guard, and his duties include monitoring the exit alarm and questioning customers suspected of shoplifting.

On a busy Saturday, Mei, a 34-year-old nurse, walks out through the exit gates carrying her handbag and a jacket she had bought at another shopping centre that morning. The alarm sounds. Trent walks up behind her and taps her on the shoulder, saying “Excuse me, madam.” Mei turns and says, “Sorry, I’m late for my shift,” and keeps walking. Trent steps in front of her, raises his clenched fist to chest height and says loudly, “Come back inside quietly or I’ll put you on the floor.” Mei later says she was “more embarrassed than frightened.” Trent then grabs Mei’s wrist and pulls her about 20 metres through the store, in front of dozens of shoppers, into a small back office. He takes her handbag and empties it onto the desk. Nothing from Bargain Barn is found. It is later discovered that the jacket still had another retailer’s security tag on it.

Trent then tells Mei: “You stay here until my manager gets back from lunch. You’re not going anywhere.” He locks the office door from the outside. The only other exit is a small window that opens onto the loading dock three metres below. Mei is held for 90 minutes before the manager returns and lets her go.

Mei has bruising to her wrist (her GP visit cost $120), missed her shift ($420 in lost wages), and says she has been humiliated, as several of her patients’ families saw her being dragged through the store.

Advise Mei whether she can bring an action under Tort Law against Bargain Barn. 25 marks

Answer plan (5 lines)
  1. Opener: battery, assault, false imprisonment (+ trespass to goods briefly) → defences → vicarious liability → remedies.
  2. Battery: tap = Rixon (not battery) vs wrist-grab and drag = battery (Battiato); defence of property not proportionate.
  3. Assault: fist + conditional threat = Stephens v Myers, Rozsa; “not frightened” = Brady v Schatzel.
  4. False imprisonment: locked office; window = Burton v Davies not Bird v Jones; Symes; justification ends after search.
  5. Vicarious liability (Century, Deatons); remedies: ordinary ($120 + $420), aggravated (humiliation), possibly exemplary.

Whether Mei can recover against Bargain Barn under Tort Law depends upon whether its security guard, Trent, committed the torts of battery, assault and false imprisonment (and, briefly, trespass to goods), whether any defence applies, and whether Bargain Barn is vicariously liable for Trent’s conduct.

Margin note: the opener lists every issue in the order you’ll deal with them. Markers like seeing the whole map up front.

Trespass to the person: general principles

A tort is a civil wrong that allows the aggrieved person to sue the wrongdoer for damages. Trespass to the person is an intentional tort, and it takes three forms: battery, assault and false imprisonment. Trespass is “actionable per se”, so Mei does not need to prove actual damage. Each form requires an interference that is direct, intentional or negligent, and without consent or lawful justification.

Issue 1: Battery

Issue: Can Mei bring an action against Bargain Barn for the tort of battery?

Law: A person commits battery if (1) they cause some physical interference with another person’s body, (2) the act is direct, (3) the act is intentional or negligent, and (4) there is no consent or lawful justification. In Battiato v Lagana [1992], Moynihan J stated that “the direct intentional imposition of any unwanted physical contact on another person constitutes the tort of Battery”, and that there is no requirement to prove physical harm. However, physical contact that is “generally acceptable in the ordinary conduct of everyday life” is excepted (In re F (Mental Patient: Sterilisation) [1990] 2 AC 1, Lord Goff). In Rixon v Star City Pty Ltd [2001], a banned patron was confronted by a casino employee, who placed his hand on Rixon’s shoulder to get his attention and detained him until police arrived. The court held that the contact was generally acceptable in the ordinary conduct of daily life, and Star City was not liable in battery. Defences to trespass include consent and defence of property, which requires that the trespass was reasonably necessary to protect the defendant’s goods from imminent harm by the plaintiff and was proportionate to the threat.

Application:

The shoulder tap. Trent’s initial tap on Mei’s shoulder while saying “Excuse me, madam” is almost identical to the contact in Rixon. It was made only to get Mei’s attention after the alarm sounded and is the kind of contact generally acceptable in everyday life. The tap alone is therefore unlikely to be a battery.

The wrist grab and drag. Trent’s later conduct is quite different. He physically interfered with Mei’s body by grabbing her wrist and pulling her about 20 metres through the store. Following Battiato v Lagana, this unwanted contact is a battery whether or not it caused harm, although here it also caused bruising. Bargain Barn may argue that, as in Rixon, the contact was a necessary part of detaining a suspect. However, Rixon is distinguishable: there the employee merely rested a hand on the patron’s shoulder, while Trent forcibly gripped and dragged Mei. That goes well beyond contact generally acceptable in the ordinary conduct of daily life. The contact was direct (Trent’s own hand) and plainly intentional.

Consent and lawful justification. Mei did not consent; she had already said she was leaving for her shift. Bargain Barn may argue defence of property, since the alarm gave Trent grounds to suspect theft. However, that defence requires the force to be reasonably necessary and proportionate to an imminent threat. Mei was not running, resisting or acting aggressively, and Trent could have asked her to show her bag or simply checked the jacket’s tag at the gate. Dragging her 20 metres was disproportionate. The facts don’t mention any statutory power of arrest, and the course materials don’t address one, so I assume no further justification is available.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Trent’s shoulder tap was not a battery, but his grabbing and dragging of Mei was.

Margin note: arguing “for” with Rixon and then distinguishing it is exactly the case-comparison move the W7 exemplar rewards.

Issue 2: Assault

Issue: Can Mei bring an action against Bargain Barn for the tort of assault?

Law: A person commits assault if (1) they cause another person to develop a reasonable apprehension of direct, imminent and harmful or offensive physical contact, (2) the act is direct, (3) the act is intentional or negligent, and (4) there is no consent or lawful justification. No physical contact needs to occur. In Brady v Schatzel [1911] it was held that it is not material that the person assaulted was actually put in fear, as otherwise the question would depend on whether the victim was a courageous or timid person. If the defendant makes carrying out a threat conditional on the plaintiff complying with a demand, the tort is still committed (Rozsa v Samuels [1969]). In Stephens v Myers (1830), a defendant who advanced towards the plaintiff with a clenched fist, intending to strike, but was stopped before reaching him, was liable in assault.

Application: Trent stepped in front of Mei, raised his clenched fist to chest height and said loudly, “Come back inside quietly or I’ll put you on the floor.” Similar to Stephens v Myers, where advancing with a clenched fist was enough, Trent was face-to-face with Mei and capable of carrying out the threat immediately. A reasonable person in Mei’s position would apprehend imminent harmful contact. Bargain Barn may argue the threat was conditional, since Trent would only act if Mei refused to come back. However, under Rozsa v Samuels, a threat conditional on the plaintiff complying with a demand is still an assault. Bargain Barn may also rely on Mei’s comment that she was “more embarrassed than frightened.” Under Brady v Schatzel this is immaterial: the test is whether the apprehension of contact was reasonable, not whether Mei was actually afraid. The threat was direct and intentional, and Mei posed no physical threat to Trent, so self-defence cannot apply.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Trent committed assault against Mei.

Issue 3: False imprisonment

Issue: Can Mei bring an action against Bargain Barn for the tort of false imprisonment?

Law: A person commits false imprisonment if (1) they cause another person to be totally restrained, meaning there is no reasonable avenue of escape, (2) the act is direct, (3) the act is intentional or negligent, and (4) there is no consent or lawful justification. Physical force is not necessary. In Symes v Mahon [1922] SASR 447, a police officer told the plaintiff he had a warrant for his arrest and he had to accompany him to Adelaide. The plaintiff did so, was found not to be the right person, and successfully sued for false imprisonment. The restraint must be total: in Bird v Jones (1845), a person denied passage on one side of a bridge was held to be merely “obstructed”, because the means of escape must be reasonable, without risk of injury or serious inconvenience. In Burton v Davies (1953), a hitch-hiker who was groped and refused permission to leave a truck, and whose only means of escape was to jump from the moving vehicle, was falsely imprisoned, because that escape carried a risk of injury.

Application:

Total restraint. Mei was dragged into the office, told “You’re not going anywhere,” and locked in for 90 minutes. Even before the door was locked, Trent’s assertion that she must stay is, as in Symes v Mahon, a restraint, because Mei submitted to an assertion of authority and not by her own free choice.

Means of escape. Bargain Barn may argue that, as in Bird v Jones, Mei was not totally restrained because she could have left through the window. However, Bird v Jones is distinguishable: the plaintiff there could simply take another route across the bridge without danger. Here the only alternative exit was a small window three metres above a loading dock. Like jumping from the moving truck in Burton v Davies, climbing out carried a clear risk of injury, so it was not a reasonable means of escape. Mei was therefore totally restrained.

Direct and intentional. Trent personally locked the door and told Mei she must stay, so the restraint was direct and deliberate.

Consent and lawful justification. Mei did not consent. Bargain Barn may argue that a short detention to investigate the alarm was justified to protect its goods. Even if a brief stop at the exit could be justified as proportionate, any justification ended once Trent emptied Mei’s handbag and found nothing from Bargain Barn. Locking her in for a further 90 minutes only because the manager was at lunch was not reasonably necessary to protect any goods and was disproportionate.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Mei was falsely imprisoned, at least for the 90 minutes after nothing was found in her handbag.

Issue 4: Trespass to goods (brief)

Issue: Can Mei bring an action against Bargain Barn for trespass to goods?

Law: Trespass to goods is a direct, intentional or negligent interference with goods in the plaintiff’s possession, such as taking or using them without permission, without consent or lawful justification. No damage need be proved.

Application: Trent took Mei’s handbag and emptied it onto the desk without her consent while she was being held, which is a direct and deliberate interference with goods in her possession.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, this is a trespass to goods, although because no damage was caused, only nominal damages are likely.

Issue 5: Is Bargain Barn vicariously liable?

Law: An employer may be liable for the acts or omissions of its employees, considering whether the act occurred as part of the employee’s authorised work (Century v Northern Ireland (1942)) and within the course of employment (Deatons v Flew (1949)).

Application: Trent is employed by Bargain Barn as a security guard, and monitoring the exit alarm and questioning suspected shoplifters is exactly the work he is authorised to do. Although he carried out that work in a wrongful way, he did so during his shift, in the store and to protect Bargain Barn’s stock. It was not a purely personal act unconnected with his job.

Conclusion: Bargain Barn is likely to be vicariously liable for Trent’s torts.

Remedies

The typical remedy in tort is damages, which aim to restore Mei, so far as money can, to the position she would have been in had the torts not been committed. Ordinary damages are assessed once and for all. Mei should claim ordinary damages for her GP visit ($120) and lost wages ($420). She should also claim aggravated damages, which compensate for humiliation and emotional distress: she was dragged through a busy store in front of dozens of shoppers, including families of her patients. Because Trent continued to hold Mei for 90 minutes after finding nothing, she may argue for exemplary damages on the basis that her rights were deliberately disregarded, although this is less certain, as the facts suggest carelessness rather than malice. Nominal damages are available for the trespass to her handbag.

Overall conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Mei can recover damages from Bargain Barn for battery, assault and false imprisonment (and nominally for trespass to goods), including aggravated damages for her humiliation.

Margin note: if the question had said Mei broke her wrist when Trent tackled her, you would add negligence as a separate Issue using T1, or simply bolt Battery A, Assault B and FI C onto a negligence answer.

Practice Question B · The brewery next door (private nuisance + trespass to land)

Riverside Retreat Pty Ltd owns and operates a 20-room boutique hotel on Mill Street, Toowong, which it has owned since 2015. The street is zoned mixed-use: there are cafés, offices and apartments, and Riverside Retreat promotes itself as a “quiet riverside escape.”

In February 2026, Hopworks Brewing Pty Ltd leased the old warehouse next door and opened a craft brewery and bar. Since March, Hopworks has hosted live bands on its outdoor deck every Thursday to Sunday until 1:00 am, with the bass clearly audible and the windows vibrating in guest rooms on that side of the hotel. Hopworks also vents its brewing exhaust through a fan pointed directly towards the hotel, and guests regularly complain of a strong, sour “boiled grain” smell in the courtyard during the day.

Riverside Retreat’s manager, Grace, complained in writing to Hopworks’ owner, Marco, in March, April and June. Marco replied: “This is a precinct, not a library. Your guests can buy earplugs.” An acoustic engineer quoted $6,000 for sound-insulating panels on the deck. Marco refused to pay for them.

In May, Hopworks installed a large illuminated sign on its wall which projects 40 centimetres over Riverside Retreat’s courtyard. Hopworks’ delivery drivers also routinely park their trucks on Riverside Retreat’s private driveway while unloading kegs, despite a “Private Property — Hotel Guests Only” sign.

Riverside Retreat has received 37 written complaints and refunded or lost bookings worth $38,500, and its online rating has dropped from 4.8 to 3.9 stars.

Advise Riverside Retreat of any actions it may have against Hopworks under Tort Law, and the remedies available. 25 marks

Answer plan (5 lines)
  1. Opener: private nuisance (noise; smell), trespass to land (sign; trucks), remedies. Public nuisance is not assessed, so leave it out.
  2. Nuisance: 5 elements; then 6 factors (severity, duration, location (both sides), sensitivity (both sides), deliberateness, precautions).
  3. Trespass to land: sign = Kelsen (40cm > 4 inches); trucks = direct entry; no consent (sign + complaints).
  4. Defences: none fit (no consent, no necessity).
  5. Remedies: damages $38,500 (ordinary), nominal for trespass if no loss, interim + final injunctions (ongoing torts).

Whether Riverside Retreat can take action against Hopworks under Tort Law depends upon two issues: whether Hopworks’ noise and exhaust odour amount to a private nuisance, and whether its overhanging sign and the parking of its trucks amount to trespass to land. If so, the remaining question is what remedies are available.

Issue 1: Private nuisance

Issue: Can Riverside Retreat bring an action against Hopworks for the tort of private nuisance?

Law: Nuisance is an act by the defendant that indirectly interferes with the plaintiff’s use and enjoyment of private or public land. It differs from trespass, which is a direct interference with the land itself. (Public nuisance is not relevant here.) A person commits private nuisance if: (1) they interfere with another person’s use and enjoyment of private land as an indirect result of their actions; (2) the other person has an interest in that land, such as an owner or tenant; (3) the other person suffers actual harm or damage; (4) the interference is intentional or reckless; and (5) the interference is sustained and unreasonable. Because there must be a balance between the right to undisturbed enjoyment of property and the right to undertake activities, the court considers the severity, duration, location, sensitivity, and deliberateness or maliciousness of the interference, and whether any precautions were taken to minimise it.

Margin note: no nuisance case is named in the course slides, so the marks come from working through the six balancing factors with the facts, on both sides.

Application:

(1) Indirect interference with use and enjoyment. The band noise, the vibrations and the brewing odour all start on Hopworks’ premises and travel onto Riverside Retreat’s land. Nothing physically enters the land, so this is indirect interference and properly nuisance rather than trespass. It interferes with Riverside Retreat’s use of its land as a hotel: guests can’t sleep in the rooms on that side, and the courtyard is unpleasant during the day.

(2) Interest in land. Riverside Retreat has owned the hotel since 2015, so it has a clear interest in the land. Note that individual guests have no interest in the land and could not sue in nuisance themselves.

(3) Actual harm. Unlike trespass, nuisance requires actual harm. Riverside Retreat has suffered $38,500 in refunded and lost bookings, 37 written complaints and a fall in its online rating from 4.8 to 3.9.

(4) Intentional or reckless. Hopworks deliberately schedules late bands and deliberately points its exhaust fan towards the hotel. In any case, after Grace’s written complaints in March, April and June, Hopworks knew of the interference and continued, which is at least reckless.

(5) Sustained and unreasonable.

  • Severity: the bass is “clearly audible” inside guest rooms and makes the windows vibrate, and the smell is described as strong. These are serious interferences, not trivial annoyances.
  • Duration: the noise occurs four nights every week until 1:00 am and has continued since March; the odour occurs regularly during the day. The interference is sustained, not a one-off event.
  • Location: Hopworks will argue, as Marco said, that Mill Street is a mixed-use “precinct” with cafés and bars, where residents and businesses must accept some noise. This is Hopworks’ strongest argument. However, the precinct also contains apartments and a hotel that has operated since 2015. Even in a mixed-use area, amplified bass until 1:00 am four nights a week goes beyond the ordinary noise expected there.
  • Sensitivity: Hopworks may argue that a hotel marketed as a “quiet riverside escape” is unusually sensitive to noise. However, needing to sleep at night is not an abnormal sensitivity: any residential neighbour would be affected in the same way. The odour also affects the courtyard during the day, which isn’t a matter of special sensitivity.
  • Deliberateness or maliciousness: Marco’s dismissive response (“Your guests can buy earplugs”) and the decision to keep the exhaust fan pointed at the hotel show a deliberate disregard for Riverside Retreat’s interests.
  • Precautions: Hopworks took no precautions at all. It refused a $6,000 insulation solution, which is modest compared with the $38,500 loss caused, and did nothing to redirect its exhaust.

Balancing these factors, although Hopworks has a legitimate right to run a brewery in a mixed-use area, the severity, duration, deliberateness and complete absence of precautions outweigh the location argument. The interference is therefore sustained and unreasonable.

Conclusion: On the balance of probabilities, Hopworks’ late-night noise and exhaust odour constitute a private nuisance against Riverside Retreat.

Issue 2: Trespass to land

Issue: Can Riverside Retreat bring an action against Hopworks for the tort of trespass to land?

Law: Trespass to land occurs where the defendant unlawfully enters, remains on, or puts something on the plaintiff’s land. A person commits trespass to land if: (1) they interfere with another person’s exclusive possession of land; (2) the interference is a direct result of the defendant’s actions; (3) the interference is intentional or, though not intended, negligent (a voluntary act); and (4) there is no consent or lawful justification. Trespass is actionable per se, so no damage needs to be proved. In Kelsen v Imperial Tobacco [1957] 2 QB 334, Imperial Tobacco erected a sign that protruded four inches into the airspace above premises it had leased to Kelsen. The court held this was a trespass and granted an injunction to prevent it. Defences include consent and necessity, i.e. that the trespass was necessary to protect life, land or goods from imminent and real harm.

Application:

(a) The illuminated sign. Riverside Retreat, as owner, has exclusive possession of its courtyard and the airspace above it. Hopworks’ sign projects 40 centimetres over the courtyard. Similar to Kelsen, where a protrusion of only four inches was a trespass, this is an interference with Riverside Retreat’s airspace, and at 40 centimetres it is a much larger intrusion than in Kelsen. The interference is direct, because Hopworks itself installed the sign, and intentional, because it chose the sign’s size and position. Even if Hopworks did not realise the sign crossed the boundary, installing it was a voluntary act, so it was at least negligent.

(b) The delivery trucks. Hopworks’ drivers physically enter and park on Riverside Retreat’s private driveway while unloading. This is a direct entry onto the land, and it is deliberate. Riverside Retreat may need to show the drivers are Hopworks’ employees, or are acting on its instructions, for Hopworks to be responsible; the facts call them “Hopworks’ delivery drivers”, so I assume they are employees acting in the course of their employment (Deatons v Flew (1949)).

(c) Consent or lawful justification. Riverside Retreat never consented. The “Private Property — Hotel Guests Only” sign expressly denies permission, and Grace’s written complaints show clear objection. Necessity does not apply: parking on the driveway for convenience is not needed to protect life, land or goods from imminent and real harm.
